A Weiss–Williams theorem for spaces of embeddings and the homotopy type of spaces of long knots
Samuel Muñoz-Echániz

Abstract
. We establish a pseudoisotopy result for embedding spaces in the line of that of Weiss and Williams for diffeomorphism groups. In other words, for P ⊂ M P\subset M a codimension at least three embedding, we describe the difference in a range of homotopical degrees between the spaces of block and ordinary embeddings of P P into M M as a certain infinite loop space involving the relative algebraic K K -theory of the pair ( M , M − P ) (M,M-P) . This range of degrees is the so-called concordance embedding stable range, which, by recent developments of Goodwillie–Krannich–Kupers, is far beyond that of the aforementioned theorem of Weiss–Williams. We use this result to give a full description of the homotopy type (away from 2 and up to the concordance embedding stable range) of the space of long knots of codimension at least 3. In doing so, we carry out an extensive analysis of certain geometric involutions in algebraic K K -theory that may be of independent interest.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
57R40, 57S05, 58D10, 18F50, 19D10, 57R80

1.
Introduction

The classical approach to study the homotopy type of the diffeomorphism group
\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M)
of a compact, possibly with boundary, high-dimensional manifold
M^{d}
(i.e.
d\geq 5
) is based on the so called
surgery-pseudoisotopy
program, which focuses on the homotopy fibre sequence

(1.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}/\mathrm{Diff})_{\partial}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

The right-hand term is the classifying space of the simplicial group
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)_{\bullet}
of
block diffeomorphisms
of
M
(cf. Definition
2.8
), an approximation to the ordinary diffeomorphism group of
M
that closely resembles the behaviour of the topological monoid
h\mathrm{Aut}_{\partial}(M)
of homotopy automorphisms of
M
; for instance, one of the defining properties of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(-)
is that it satisfies a natural equivalence
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M\times I)\simeq\Omega\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)
, which also holds for
h\mathrm{Aut}_{\partial}(-)
(but is very much not true for
\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(-)
).
Surgery theory
, as developed by Browder, Novikov, Ranicki, Sullivan, Wall, et al., roughly studies the difference between
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)
and
h\mathrm{Aut}_{\partial}(M)
in terms of the mapping space
(G/O)_{*}^{(M,\partial M)}
and the
L
-theory of the group ring
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}[\pi_{1}(M)]
, making the homotopy type of
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)
theoretically accessible via homotopy theory and
L
-theory.

It is in understanding the homotopy type of
(\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}/\mathrm{Diff})_{\partial}(M)
, the homotopy fibre of the map
i
, where
pseudoisotopy theory

[
Igu88
,
HW73
]
comes into play. Originally, this theory was concerned with the study of the topological group
C(M)
of
concordances
or
pseudoisotopies
of
M
consisting of diffeomorphisms of
M\times I
that restrict to the identity on a neighbourhood of
M\times\{0\}\cup\partial M\times I
; in other words, pseudoisotopies of
M
are precisely the automorphisms of the trivial
h
-cobordism starting at
M
. The spaces
C(M)
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}/\mathrm{Diff}(M)
are intimately related, as was first made precise by Hatcher
[
Hat78
, Prop. 2.1]
through a spectral sequence. Hatcher’s realisation eventually evolved into the following celebrated theorem of Weiss and Williams
[
WW88
, Thm. A]
, which will be of central importance all throughout this paper.

Theorem 1.1

(Weiss–Williams)
.

Let
M^{d}
be a compact smooth
d
-manifold. There exists a map

\Phi^{\mathrm{Diff}}:(\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}/\mathrm{Diff})_{\partial}(M)\longrightarrow\Omega^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{H}^{s}(M)_{hC_{2}}\right)

which is
(\phi(d)+1)
-connected, where
\phi(d)
denotes the concordance stable range of dimension
d
(which by Igusa’s theorem
[
Igu88
]
is at least
\min(\frac{d-4}{3},\frac{d-7}{2})
).

In this theorem, the
C_{2}
-spectrum
\mathbf{H}^{s}(M)
is the
1
-connective cover of a spectrum
\mathbf{H}(M)
which is roughly built out of deloopings of spaces of smooth
h
-cobordisms (cf. Notation
2.6
and Section
5.2
), and whose involution corresponds (up to a minus sign) to ”reversing the direction of an
h
-cobordism”. This latter spectrum has a close connection to algebraic
K
-theory, as seen by the split fibre sequence of spaces
[
WJR13a
]

(1.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{H}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{+}M:=\Omega^{\infty}\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}M,}

where
A(M)
denotes Waldhausen’s
A
-theory space of
M
(cf.
[
Wal85
]
). Even though the homotopy types of
A(M)
and its involutions are difficult to understand in general, much can be said when
M
is homotopy equivalent to a point
[
Rog02
,
Rog03
,
BM19
]
or the circle
[
Hes09
]
, or when working rationally
[
BF86
]
.

1.1.
The surgery-pseudoisotopy program for spaces of embeddings

The homotopy type of embedding spaces is intrinsically tied to that of diffeomorphism groups as seen via the
isotopy extension theorem
. More precisely, let
M^{d}
be as before and let
\iota:P\xhookrightarrow{}M
be a compact submanifold that meets
\partial M
transversely. Write
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)
for the space of smooth embeddings of
P
into
M
which agree with
\iota
in a neighbourhood of
\partial_{0}P:=P\cap\partial M
and send
\partial P-\partial_{0}P
to the interior of
M
. Then there is a homotopy fibre sequence

(1.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M-\nu P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M),}

where
\nu P
is a small tubular neighbourhood of the standard embedding
\iota:P\xhookrightarrow{}M
, and the subscript
\langle\iota\rangle
stands for the components of the embedding space that contain the orbit of the action of
\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M)
on
\iota
by postcomposition. In this sense, embedding spaces are the corresponding ”relative analogues” of diffeomorphism groups, and often their homotopy types become easier to study.

In this paper we would like to advertise a direct approach for studying the homotopy type of embedding spaces (in a range of degrees) which is analogous to the one for diffeomorphism groups previously surveyed. As before, one first analyses the space of block embeddings
\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)
via relative surgery methods; the main result in this direction is due to Browder–Casson–Sullivan–Wall (cf.
[
GKW01
, Thm. 2.2.1]
), and asserts that, as long as the codimension of
P\subset M
is at least
3
, then the space of block embeddings is the homotopy pullback of a diagram involving so called
Poincaré block embeddings
and
immersions
and ordinary block immersions. Due to the Smale–Hirsch immersion theorem, all the ingredients that come into the mix are accessible through homotopy theory and thus, up to extensions, so are block embeddings.

Following the same strategy as for the classical surgery-pseudoisotopy program, it remains to understand the difference between ordinary and block embeddings, i.e. the homotopy fibre

(1.4)

\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M):=\operatorname{hofib}_{\iota}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)\to\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)),

by means of pseudoisotopy theory. This space also fits in another homotopy fibre sequence

(1.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}/\mathrm{Diff})_{\partial}(M-\nu P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}/\mathrm{Diff})_{\partial}(M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

obtained as the fibre of the map from (
1.3
) to its block analogue (see (
3.4
)). What was previously fulfilled by Theorem
1.1
for the pseudoisotopy part of diffeomorphism groups seems to be missing in the case of embedding spaces; the best result known in this direction is
Morlet’s lemma of disjunction

[
BLR06
, Thm. 3.1]
which, in that reformulation, determines the connectivity of the map
\mu
.

Our first main result fills in this gap in the surgery-pseudoisotopy program for embedding spaces, and describes the homotopy type of
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}^{(\sim)}(P,M)
in a range outside of the connectivity of
\mu
.

Theorem A
.

There exists a map

\Phi^{\operatorname{Emb}}:\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial}(P,M)\longrightarrow\Omega^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{CE}(P,M)_{hC_{2}}\right)

which is
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)
-connected if the handle dimension
p
of
P
relative to
\partial_{0}P
satisfies
p\leq d-3
. Here
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}
is the concordance embedding stable range (see (
1.6
)) and

\mathbf{CE}(P,M):=\operatorname{hofib}(\mathbf{H}(M-\nu P)\to\mathbf{H}(M)).

Remark 1.2
.

The involutions in the
h
-cobordism spectra involved in the statement of Theorem
A
are exactly those of Theorem
1.1
, which naturally arise from Weiss’ orthogonal calculus (see Section
2.1
and Remark
2.1
). When
M
is stably paralellisable (and localising away from
2
), we relate these involutions to well-known algebraic ones in Theorem
5.11
and Corollary
5.15
(cf. Notation
5.1
for conventions). See also Corollary
5.8
for the effect of these involutions on
\pi_{0}^{s}(\mathbf{H}(M))=\mathrm{Wh}(\pi_{1}(M))
in terms of Milnor’s involution
[
Mil66
]
.

Remark 1.3

(Topological version of Theorem
A
)
.

As stated, Theorem
A
only applies to smooth embeddings, but an analogous statement in
CAT=\mathrm{Top}
also holds after some adjustments: Firstly, the smooth embedding spaces should be replaced by the spaces of locally flat topological embeddings, and the
h
-cobordism spectra by their topological analogues. Moreover, the result is only valid when
P
has geometric codimension zero in
M
; this is because a topological analogue of Proposition
3.3
cannot be true, as we explain in Remark
3.4
. One should bear in mind, however, that the bound (
1.7
) is a priori only valid for smoothable topological manifolds. See also Remarks
3.6
,
3.2
and
A.5
for modified arguments in the topological setting.

There are two remarkable features of Theorem
A
that make this result specially suitable for computational purposes. Let us comment on these now.

1.1.1.
The concordance embedding stable range

Fix
\iota:P\xhookrightarrow{}M
as before. A
concordance embedding
of
P
into
M
is an embedding
\varphi:P\times I\xhookrightarrow{}M\times I
such that

(
a
)

\varphi^{-1}(M\times\{i\})=P\times\{i\}
for
i=0,1
and

(
b
)

\varphi
agrees with the inclusion
\iota\times\mathrm{Id}_{I}
on a neighbourhood of
P\times\{0\}\cup\partial_{0}P\times I
.

We denote by
C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M)
the space of all such embeddings, topologised as a subspace of
\operatorname{Emb}(P\times I,M\times I)
. There are stabilisation maps
\Sigma_{(M,P)}:C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M)\to C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times I,M\times I)
given by taking the product of an embedding with
I
and unbending corners appropriately (see
[
GKK23
, Fig. 1]
), and the
concordance embedding stable range
for
(d,p)
is

(1.6)

\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p):=\max\left\{k\in\mathbb{N}\;\middle|\;\begin{aligned} &\Sigma_{(M,P)}\ \text{is $k$-connected for all $(M,P)$}\\
&\text{with $\dim M=d$ and {h}-$\dim(P,\partial_{0}P)=p$}\end{aligned}\right\}.

Goodwillie–Krannich–Kupers
[
GKK23
]
have recently shown that if
p\leq d-3
, then

(1.7)

\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)\geq 2d-p-5,

which is far beyond Igusa’s lower bound for the concordance stable range
\phi(d)
. In the concordance stable range
\phi(d)
, Theorem
A
is a consequence of Theorem
1.1
and the isotopy extension sequence (
1.5
), so our main contribution is improving the connectivity of the map
\Phi^{\operatorname{Emb}}
to the concordance embedding stable range
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)
. We will also see in Remark
6.6
that the lower bound of (
1.7
) is the best one could do (this was already apparent in
[
GKK23
]
).

1.1.2.
Relative algebraic
K
-theory via trace methods

Given a map of spaces
Y\to X
, let
A(Y\to X)
denote the homotopy fibre of the induced map
A(Y)\to A(X)
. By (
1.2
), there is an equivalence of spaces

A(M-P\to M)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{CE}(P,M)\times\Omega Q(M/M-P).

The handle codimension condition on the embedding
\iota:P\subset M
in the statement of Theorem
A
guarantees that the inclusion
M-P\to M
is
2
-connected. This can be used to our advantage, as firstly it ensures that the spectrum
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
is connective (see Lemma
3.12
), but more importantly that, via
trace methods
, the homotopy type of
A(M-P\to M)
is far more accessible than that of
A(M-P)
and
A(M)
on their own.

Trace methods
are concerned with the study of
topological cyclic homology

[
BHM93
]
, denoted
TC(-)
, and related invariants as an approximation to algebraic
K
-theory. This is something sensible to do by the seminal work of Dundas–Goodwillie–McCarthy
[
DM94
,
Dun97
,
DGM12
]
, who showed that the cyclotomic trace map provides an equivalence of relative theories
A(Y\to X)\simeq TC(Y\to X)
, so long as
Y\to X
is
2
-connected. The treatment of TC by Nikolaus–Scholze
[
NS18
]
provides even further computational control of this invariant. In the cases we are concerned with (spherical group rings), the homotopy type of TC was fully described by Bökstedt–Hsiang–Madsen
[
BHM93
]
in terms of the stable homotopy of the free loop space
L(-):=\mathrm{Map}(S^{1},-)
together with its natural
S^{1}
-action and cyclotomic structure. When working over the field of rational numbers, this whole story simplifies even further by Goodwillie’s isomorphism
[
Goo86
]

(1.8)

\pi_{*}(A(Y\to X))\otimes\mathbb{Q}\cong HC^{-}_{*}(\Omega X,\Omega Y;\mathbb{Q})\cong H^{S^{1}}_{*}(LX,LY;\mathbb{Q}),

where
HC^{-}_{*}
denotes Connes’ negative cyclic homology, and
H^{S^{1}}_{*}
stands for the
S^{1}
-equivariant homology.

So as we have just seen, the homotopy type of (the infinite loop space of) the connective spectrum
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
of Theorem
A
is pretty accessible in general. However, one still needs to deal with the involution appearing in the statement in order to apply the result, which is a rather technical task that, so far, had only been carried out rationally by Bustamante–Farrell–Jiang
[
BFJ20
]
(they relate this involution to one on the right hand side of (
1.8
)). Integrally, one has to proceed with more care; our analysis in Section
5
deals with this issue localised away from
2
and when
M
is stably paralellisable.

1.2.
The homotopy type of spaces of long knots

The homology and homotopy of
spaces of long knots

\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
has been subject to extensive reasearch in recent years, especially through the lens of embedding calculus and its relation to the little disks operad and graph complexes. See for instance Volić
[
Vol06
]
, Watanabe
[
Wat07
]
, Sinha
[
Sin09
]
, Budney and Cohen
[
Bud08
,
BC09
]
for when
p=1
and
d=3,4
mainly, or more modern treatments as in Arone–Turchin
[
AT14
,
AT15
]
, Dwyer–Hess
[
DH12
]
, Boavida de Brito–Weiss
[
BdBW18
]
, at last culminating in the work of Fresse–Turchin–Willwacher
[
FTW17
]
where a complete answer of
\pi_{*}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d}))\otimes\mathbb{Q}
is given in terms of the homology of the
hairy graph complex
.

The second main result of this paper is a full description of the homotopy type of
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
for
d-p\geq 3
roughly in the concordance embedding stable range (
1.6
) and localised away from
2
. This is done by analysing the homotopy fibre sequence

(1.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})}

following the surgery-pseudoisotopy program for embedding spaces surveyed in Section
1.1
, a crucial step of which is Theorem
A
. Given a finite dimensional virtual
G
-representation
\rho
over

\textstyle\mathbb{R}

, denote by
\mathbb{S}^{\rho}
the representation sphere spectrum associated to it; we will consider its homotopy orbit spectrum
\mathbb{S}^{\rho}_{hG}
, which is equivalent to the Thom spectrum of the associated virtual vector bundle
EG\times_{G}\rho\to BG
. Let
\psi_{m}
denote the real
m
-dimensional representation of the dihedral group
D_{m}
(seen as a subgroup of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{m}
) given by permuting the factors of
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{m}
, and let
\sigma:C_{2}=\{\pm 1\}\xhookrightarrow{}{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{\times}
be the sign representation (also regarded as a
D_{m}
-representation by restriction along the determinant
D_{m}\xhookrightarrow{}O(2)\overset{\det}{\to}\{\pm 1\}=C_{2}
).

Theorem B
.

For
p\leq d-3
and
d\geq 5
, consider the virtual
D_{m}
-representations

\rho_{m}:=(d+1)(\sigma-1)+\psi_{m}\otimes(d-p-3+\sigma).

Then the homotopy fibre sequence (
1.9
), upon localising away from
2
and taking
(\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)-1)
-th Postnikov sections, takes the form

(1.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{m\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\geq$}}2}\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{p}\operatorname{hofib}\big(G(d-p)/O(d-p)\to G/O\big).}

The resulting sequence is split if
p\geq 2
, and splits after being looped once if
p=1
.

Remark 1.4
.

(
i
)  The spaces
G(n)/O(n)
and
G/O
appearing in (
1.10
) denote the homotopy fibres of the natural maps
BO(n)\to BG(n)
and
BO\to BG
, respectively, where
G(n)
is the topological grouplike monoid of self-homotopy equivalences of
S^{n-1}
, and
G
is the homotopy colimit of the suspension maps
G(n)\to G(n+1)
. Understanding the homotopy groups of these spaces roughly amounts to understanding unstable and stable homotopy groups of spheres.

(
ii
)  The space
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
is an
\mathbb{E}_{p}
-algebra, and so it can indeed be localised. When
d-p\geq 3
, it is exactly
(2d-3p-4)
-connected by work of Budney
[
Bud08
, Prop. 3.9]
. So when
2d-3p-4<0
, by
localising

\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
we really mean localising each of its connected components one at a time (and
not
localising the abelian group
\pi_{0}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d}))
directly). The same applies to the outer terms in (
1.9
).

(
iii
) When
p=1
and
d=4
(i.e. the lowest dimensional case of interest if
d-p\geq 3
), Theorem
B
holds after looping both (
1.9
) and (
1.10
); see Remark
6.1
. Using this to study the homotopy groups of
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{1},D^{4})
, however, yields weaker results than the ones in
[
Bud08
, Prop. 3.9]
.

For
A
an abelian group and
\ell
a prime, write
A_{(\ell)}:=A\otimes{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}_{(\ell)}
, i.e. the localisation of
A
at the prime
\ell
. We will compute some of the homotopy groups of the fibre in (
1.10
) in Section
6.2
, and hence deduce new torsion information about the homotopy groups of
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
in high-dimensions (i.e.
d\geq 5
).

Corollary C

(Propositions
6.5
&
6.7
)
.

For
d-p\geq 3
and
\ell
an odd prime, there are isomorphisms

\pi_{*}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d}))_{(\ell)}\cong\pi_{*+p}(\operatorname{hofib}(G(d-p)/O(d-p)\to G/O))_{(\ell)}\oplus\bigoplus_{m\geq 2}\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}

in degrees
*\leq\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)-1
. When
m\geq 2
and
\ell\nmid 2m
,

•

if
d
is even and
p
is even, then

\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\pi_{*-m(d-p-2)}^{s}\otimes{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}_{(\ell)},&m=3,5,7,\dots\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

•

if
d
is odd and
p
is odd, then

\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\pi_{*-m(d-p-2)}^{s}\otimes{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}_{(\ell)},&m=2,4,6,\dots\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

•

if
d
is even and
p
is odd, then

\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\pi_{*-m(d-p-2)}^{s}\otimes{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}_{(\ell)},&m=5,9,13,\dots\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

•

if
d
is odd and
p
is even, then

\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\pi_{*-m(d-p-2)}^{s}\otimes{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}_{(\ell)},&m=3,7,11,\dots\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

If
\ell
divides
m
, the computation of
\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{D_{m}})_{(\ell)}
must be treated case by case. When
m=\ell=3
and
d-p=3
, the first few such homotopy groups are given in Table
1
(cf. Proposition
6.7
for notation).

Rationally, this computation roughly recovers the homology of the
0
- and
1
-loop order part of the hairy graph complex appearing in
[
FTW17
, Eq. 2]
(see Remark
6.6
for more details).

Structure of the paper

Sections
2
and
3
will be devoted to the proof of Theorem
A
. We start by briefly reviewing Weiss’ theory of orthogonal calculus and then, in Section
2.2
, we present the orthogonal functors that will play a role in the proof of Theorem
A
. In doing this, we will have to carefully describe the topology on spaces of bounded diffeomorphisms in such a way that we can employ the machinery of orthogonal calculus in this setting. After reducing to the codimension zero case in Section
3.1
, we use the results in the preceding section to define the map
\Phi^{\operatorname{Emb}}
in Section
3.3
and analyse its connectivity in
3.4
.

As a consequence of Theorem
A
, in Section
4
we establish a splitting result (Theorem
4.2
) for embedding spaces of manifolds containing interval factors reminiscent of work of Burghelea–Lashof
[
BL82
, Cor. E]
.

Section
5
deals with the analysis of the
C_{2}
-spectra involved in the statements of Theorems
1.1
and
A
. The main results in this direction are Theorem
5.11
and Corollary
5.15
, where the involutions on these spectra are expressed (up to homotopy) in terms of the standard involution in algebraic
K
-theory.

Section
6
is devoted to Theorem
B
, whose proof is a formal consequence of the results in the preceeding sections. We then draw some conclusions on the homotopy groups of spaces of long knots in Section
6.2
.

In Appendix
A
we explore certain aspects related to spaces of bounded diffeomorphisms and embeddings. Namely in Section
A.1
we show that the topological models for these spaces introduced in Section
2.2
coincide (up to weak equivalence) with the simplicial ones of Definition
2.8
. In Section
A.2
we give a ”moduli space of manifolds” description for the classifying space of the bounded diffeomorphism group.

In Appendix
B
we show that the
h
-cobordism stabilisation map anti-commutes with the involutions in these spaces. This is analogous to a result of Hatcher
[
Hat78
, Appendix I, Lem.]
and Burghelea–Lashof
[
BL82
, Cor. A7]
for spaces of concordance diffeomorphisms.

2.
Orthogonal calculus and spaces of bounded diffeomorphisms

Much of the proof of Theorem
1.1
in
[
WW88
]
is an application of Weiss’
orthogonal calculus
but in disguise, as this theory was not yet formalised at the time. In this section we briefly review the main aspects of this theory and develop some necessary tools required for the proof of Theorem
A
.

2.1.
A quick tour through orthogonal calculus

Weiss’
orthogonal calculus

[
Wei95
]
is a calculus of functors useful to understand objects of geometric flavour. It studies
continuous
functors from the category
\mathcal{J}
of real finite-dimensional inner product vector spaces and linear isometries to the category of (compactly generated weakly Hausdorff) spaces
\mathsf{Top}
. Such a functor
F:\mathcal{J}\to\mathsf{Top}
is said to be
continuous
if the evaluation map

\mathrm{mor}_{\mathcal{J}}(U,V)\times F(U)\longrightarrow F(V)

is continuous for all
U,V\in\mathcal{J}
. Here
\mathrm{mor}_{\mathcal{J}}(U,V)
denotes the
Stiefel manifold
of linear isometries from
U
to
V
, so that
\mathcal{J}
is enriched over
\mathsf{Top}
. We will work in a slightly different setup, where
\mathsf{Top}
is replaced by the category
\mathsf{Top}_{*}
of pointed spaces and
\mathcal{J}
is replaced by the pointed topological category
\mathcal{J}_{0}
with the same objects and with

\mathrm{mor}_{\mathcal{J}_{0}}(U,V):=\mathrm{mor}_{\mathcal{J}}(U,V)_{+},

as morphism spaces. Similarly, a functor
F:\mathcal{J}_{0}\to\mathsf{Top}_{*}
is
continuous
if the evaluation map

\mathrm{mor}_{\mathcal{J}_{0}}(U,V)\wedge F(U)\longrightarrow F(V)

is continuous for all
U,V\in\mathcal{J}_{0}
. Such a functor
F(-)
is also sometimes called an
orthogonal functor
.

The machinery of orthogonal calculus associates to each such orthogonal functor
F(-)
a sequence of (naïve)
O(k)
-spectra
\Theta F^{(k)}
for
k\geq 1
—the
derivatives of

F
—which fit in a tower

(2.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{2}F(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left((S^{2\cdot(-)}\wedge\Theta F^{(2)})_{hO(2)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}F(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left((S^{1\cdot(-)}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})_{hO(1)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{0}F(-)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hskip-70.0ptF({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{\infty})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{0}}

of orthogonal functors—the
Taylor tower
. Here

•

S^{k\cdot V}
is the one point compactification of
k\cdot V:={\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{k}\otimes V
, which is acted upon
O(k)
in the
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{k}
component, and diagonally on the smash
S^{k\cdot V}\wedge\Theta F^{(k)}
,

•

the right hand horizontal maps—the
layers
—indicate the inclusions of the homotopy fibres of the subsequent vertical maps between the
stages

T_{k}F(-)
of the tower,

•

the zeroth stage
T_{0}F(-)
is given by

T_{0}F(V):=\operatorname{hocolim}_{k}F(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{k})
, and thus admits a canonical equivalence from the constant orthogonal functor with
value at infinity

F({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{\infty}):=\operatorname{hocolim}_{k}F({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{k})
. The map
\eta_{0}:F(V)\to T_{0}F(V)
is simply the inclusion map.

In the proof of Theorem
A
we will analyse the Taylor tower (
2.1
) only up to the first layer, so we shall now describe the spectrum
\Theta F^{(1)}
in detail. For
V\in\mathcal{J}_{0}
, consider
F^{(1)}(V):=\operatorname{hofib}(F(V)\to F(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}))
, the homotopy fibre of the map induced by the standard inclusion
V\to V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}
. These spaces inherit an
O(1)
-action by declaring
-1\in O(1)
to act on
V
and
V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}
by
-1
on
all
coordinates. There are
O(1)
-equivariant maps

(2.2)

s_{V}:S^{1}\wedge F^{(1)}(V)\longrightarrow F^{(1)}(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}),

given by rotating
V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{2}
about the
2
-plane
0\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{2}
(as notation suggests, here
O(1)
acts trivially on the suspension coordinate. In general, we adopt the convention that
S^{n}
denotes the
n
-sphere with the trivial
O(1)
-action). Then the
O(1)
-spectrum
\Theta F^{(1)}
has
F^{(1)}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{n})
as its
n
-th space, and
s_{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{n}}
as the structure map
S^{1}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)}_{n}\to\Theta F^{(1)}_{n+1}
.

Remark 2.1
.

This is not quite the
O(1)
-action described in
[
Wei95
, Prop. 3.1]
;
O(1)
there acts on
F^{(1)}(V)=\operatorname{hofib}(F(V)\to F(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}))
by declaring the action of
-1\in O(1)
on
V
to be trivial and by
-1
on the

\textstyle\mathbb{R}

-summand of
V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}
. If we write
\underline{F}^{(1)}(V)
for this
O(1)
-space, then the maps

(2.3)

s_{V}:S^{\sigma}\wedge\underline{F}^{(1)}(V)\longrightarrow\underline{F}^{(1)}(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}})\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{F}^{(1)}(V)\to F(V)

are
O(1)
-equivariant, where
\sigma
stands for the (
1
-dimensional) sign
O(1)
-representation and
S^{\sigma}
for its associated representation sphere. The corresponding (sequential) spectrum, call it
\underline{\Theta}F^{(1)}
, is
not
a naïve
O(1)
-spectrum in the usual sense anymore, as
O(1)
acts non-trivially on the suspension coordinates. To solve this issue, Weiss introduces in
[
Wei95
, p. 17]
the
O(1)
-spectrum
\Theta^{\#}F^{(1)}
with
n
-th
O(1)
-space

(2.4)

\Theta^{\#}F^{(1)}_{n}:=\Omega^{\infty\cdot\sigma}(S^{n}\wedge\underline{\Theta}F^{(1)})=\underset{k}{\hocolim}\ \Omega^{k\cdot\sigma}(S^{n}\wedge\underline{F}^{(1)}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{k})),

where
\Omega^{k\cdot\sigma}(-):=\mathrm{Map}_{*}(S^{k\cdot\sigma},-)
and
O(1)
acts by conjugation on this mapping space.

In order to relate the
O(1)
-spectra
\Theta F^{(1)}
and
\Theta^{\#}F^{(1)}
, we observe that
F
can be naturally upgraded to a functor
\underline{F}:\mathcal{J}_{0}^{O(1)}\to\mathsf{Top}_{*}^{O(1)}
enriched over
\mathsf{Top}_{*}
, where
\mathcal{J}_{0}^{O(1)}:=\mathrm{Fun}(O(1),\mathcal{J}_{0})
is regarded as the pointed topological category of inner product finite-dimensional
O(1)
-representations. We likewise define for
V\in\mathcal{J}_{0}^{O(1)}
the
O(1)
-space
\underline{F}^{(1)}(V):=\operatorname{hofib}(\underline{F}(V)\to\underline{F}(V\oplus\sigma))
. Now tensoring such an
O(1)
-representation with the sign representation
\sigma
gives a self-isomorphism of
\mathcal{J}_{0}^{O(1)}
denoted by
-\cdot\sigma
. One could stabilise
\underline{F}^{(1)}(-)
with respect to

\textstyle\mathbb{R}

as in (
2.3
), or with the sign representation
\sigma
, giving rise to maps

s_{a,b}:S^{a\cdot\sigma+b}\wedge\underline{F}^{(1)}(V)\longrightarrow\underline{F}^{(1)}(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{a,b}),\quad a,b\geq 0,\quad V\in\mathcal{J}_{0}^{O(1)},

where
S^{a\cdot\sigma+b}:=S^{a\cdot\sigma}\wedge S^{b}
and
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{a,b}:={\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{a}\oplus b\cdot\sigma
. We then obtain a zig-zag of maps of
O(1)
-spectra

(2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{\#}F^{(1)}:=\underset{a\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\geq$}}0}{\mathrm{hocolim}}\ \mathbb{S}^{-a\cdot\sigma}\wedge\underline{F}^{(1)}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{a})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{a,b\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\geq$}}0}{\mathrm{hocolim}}\ \mathbb{S}^{-a\cdot\sigma-b}\wedge\underline{F}^{(1)}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{a,b}),}

Proof.

We first need to argue that there is a natural weak equivalence between

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left((S(V)_{+}\wedge X)_{hO(1)}\right)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}(S(V)_{+}\wedge_{O(1)}X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(X_{hO(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left((S^{V\cdot\sigma}\wedge X)_{hO(1)}\right),}

where the first equivalence follows since the pointed

-action on

is free. The above sequence when

is the left vertical fibration in the following commutative diagram of fibre sequences:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left(S(V)_{+}\wedge_{O(1)}\Theta F^{(1)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{hofib}(T_{1}F(0)\to T_{1}F(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(\Theta F^{(1)}_{hO(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}F(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{0}F(0)=F({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left((S^{V\cdot\sigma}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})_{hO(1)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{1}F(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{0}F(V)=F({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{\infty}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

\scriptstyle\simeq

This gives rise to the required weak equivalence, which is visibly natural in

.

Clearly because of this naturality, the left square of the diagram in the statement commutes, so it remains to show that the right one is homotopy commutative too. A similar claim (but without proof) is made in

, Ex. 10.1]
, so let us give an argument of our own: without loss of generality, we may replace

so that

becomes homogeneous of degree

(see

by

so that

. Then it was shown in

, Thm. 7.3]
that there is a natural zig-zag of equivalences between

for

, where

is the

). But we have seen in Remark

that there is a zig-zag (

) of

and

. Therefore, we obtain a zig-zag of equivalences between
\operatorname{hofib}(F({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{n})\to F({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{n+1}))
and the homotopy fibre of the map
i_{n}:\Omega^{\infty}((S^{n\cdot\sigma}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)}_{hO(1)})\to\Omega^{\infty}((S^{(n+1)\cdot\sigma}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)}_{hO(1)})
. This is the zig-zag showing up in the top part of the diagram

(2.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{(1)}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{hofib}\begin{pmatrix}\Omega^{\infty}((S^{n\cdot\sigma}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})_{hO(1)})\\
\hskip 3.0pt\downarrow\scalebox{0.8}{$i_{n}$}\\
\Omega^{\infty}((S^{(n+1)\cdot\sigma}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})_{hO(1)})\end{pmatrix}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(\Sigma^{n}\Theta F^{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\big((S^{n\cdot\sigma}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})_{hO(1)}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\big((S^{(n+1)\cdot\sigma}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})_{hO(1)}\big),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{stab.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq*}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{n}}

where
\lambda
is defined as follows: given two copies
S^{n}_{i}
of the
n
-sphere for
i=0,1
(with the trivial
O(1)
-action), there is an
O(1)
-map
a:S^{n}_{0}\vee S^{n}_{1}\to S^{n\cdot\sigma}
that sends
v\in S^{n}_{i}
to
(-1)^{i}v\in S^{n\cdot\sigma}
. Then

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{n}\Theta F^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{n}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((S^{n}_{0}\vee S^{n}_{1})\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})_{hO(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{n\cdot\sigma}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})_{hO(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a\wedge\mathrm{Id})_{hO(1)}}

gives rise to (the zig-zag)
\lambda
upon taking infinite loop spaces. Identifying
S^{(n+1)\cdot\sigma}
as the homotopy cofibre of the map
a
(really
S^{n+1}\vee S^{n+1}
is the cofibre of the inclusion
S^{n\cdot\sigma}\to S^{(n+1)\cdot\sigma}
), we see that
\lambda
lifts, uniquely up to homotopy, to the weak equivalence
\widehat{\lambda}
. As the top zig-zag of (
2.8
) in place of the map ”stab.” in the diagram from the statement would make its right square commutive by construction, we must show that the upper triangle of (
2.8
) is homotopy commutative. For this, it suffices to argue that the bigger square that contains it commutes up to homotopy.

One verifies from the proofs of
[
Wei95
, Prop. 7.2 & Thm. 7.3]
and the construction of (
2.5
) that the top-right composition of the square in (
2.8
) becomes, up to homotopy, the top-right composition of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{(1)}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{n})\overset{(!)}{=}\underline{F}^{(1)}_{n,0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\underline{F}^{(1)}_{n,0})_{hO(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\underset{k}{\hocolim}\ \Omega^{k}(\underline{F}^{(1)}_{n,k})\right)_{hO(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left(\Omega^{\infty}(S^{n\cdot\sigma}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})\right)_{hO(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{k}{\hocolim}\ \Omega^{k}(\underline{F}^{(1)}_{n,k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(S^{n\cdot\sigma}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left((S^{n\cdot\sigma}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})_{hO(1)}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(\Sigma^{n}\Theta F^{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(S^{n}\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left(((S^{n}\vee S^{n})\wedge\Theta F^{(1)})_{hO(1)}\right).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k=0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k=0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{n,0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(!)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(!)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(*)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a\wedge\mathrm{Id})_{hO(1)}}

Here
\underline{F}^{(1)}_{a,b}=\underline{F}^{(1)}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{a,b})=\underline{F}^{(1)}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{a}\oplus b\cdot\sigma)
and the decoration (!) indicates that the equality is not (supposed to be)
O(1)
-equivariant. All subtriangles and squares, except possibly the bottom right one labelled by (
*
), are visibly commutative. Moreover the left-bottom composition is the left-bottom composition of the square in (
2.8
) by definition. The bottom right subsquare (
*
) is commutative because the following is for every
O(1)
-spectrum
X
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{n\cdot\sigma}\wedge X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{n\cdot\sigma}\wedge X)_{hO(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S^{n}\wedge X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{n\cdot\sigma}_{0}\vee S^{n\cdot\sigma}_{1})\wedge_{O(1)}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((S^{n\cdot\sigma}_{0}\vee S^{n\cdot\sigma}_{1})\wedge X)_{hO(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(S^{n}_{0}\vee S^{n}_{1})\wedge_{O(1)}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((S^{n}_{0}\vee S^{n}_{1})\wedge X)_{hO(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(!)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c\wedge\mathrm{Id})_{hO(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\wedge\mathrm{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b\wedge\mathrm{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a\wedge\mathrm{Id})_{hO(1)}}

Here the
O(1)
-equivariant isomorphism
b:S^{n}_{0}\wedge S^{n}_{1}\to S^{n\cdot\sigma}_{0}\vee S^{n\cdot\sigma}_{1}
sends
v\in S^{n}_{i}
to
(-1)^{i}v\in S^{n\cdot\sigma}_{i}
, and
c:S^{n\cdot\sigma}_{0}\vee S^{n\cdot\sigma}_{1}\to S^{n\cdot\sigma}
is the identity in each wedge summand. This finishes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Convention 2.3
.

From now on,
F^{(1)}(V)
stands for
\underline{F}^{(1)}(V\cdot\sigma):=\operatorname{hofib}(\underline{F}(V\cdot\sigma)\to\underline{F}((V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}})\cdot\sigma))
in the notation of Remark
2.1
, unless we explictly say otherwise. This way (
2.2
) is
O(1)
-equivariant.

2.2.
The orthogonal functors of bounded diffeomorphisms

All throughout, let
\iota:P\xhookrightarrow{}M
be as in the statement of Theorem
A
. In this section we present the orthogonal functors that will play a role in the proof of Theorem
A
. These are built out of spaces of bounded diffeomorphisms, for which we will present point-set topological models that agree up to weak equivalence with the more classical simplicial ones.

Let
V\in\mathcal{J}
be an inner product finite-dimensional real vector space with associated norm
\left\lVert-\right\rVert_{V}
, and let
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
be smooth (possibly non-compact) manifolds equipped with proper maps
\pi:Q\to V
and
\pi^{\prime}:Q^{\prime}\to V
. For
t\geq 0
, a smooth map
f:Q\to Q^{\prime}
is said to be
t-bounded
if the set
\{\left\lVert\pi^{\prime}(\hskip 1.0ptf(q))-\pi(q)\right\rVert_{V}:q\in Q\}\subset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}
is bounded by
t
. More generally,
f
is
bounded
if it is
t
-bounded for some
t\geq 0
. If
Q=N\times V
for some compact manifold
N
,
\pi
will be assumed to be the projection to
V
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
V\in\mathcal{J}
. The
space of bounded diffeomorphisms
of
M\times V
relative to
\partial M\times V
is

\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times V):=\{(t,\phi)\in[0,\infty)\times\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M\times V):\text{$\phi$ is $t$-bounded}\},

endowed with the subspace topology inhereted from the product
[0,\infty)\times\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M\times V)
. Here
\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M\times V)
is endowed with the Whitney
C^{\infty}
-topology. It is a group-like topological monoid under the rule

(t,\phi)\cdot(t^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}):=(t+t^{\prime},\phi\circ\phi^{\prime}).

Example 2.5
.

Orthogonal calculus was largely inspired by the work of Weiss–Williams in
[
WW88
]
, as can be seen in
[
WW88
, Digr. 3.8]
. For
U\in\mathcal{J}_{0}
, let
B\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}^{b}(M\times U)
be the classifying space of the group-like topological monoid just introduced. Denote by
B(-)
the orthogonal functor given by
B(U):=B\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times U)
and, for
i:U\to V
a morphism in
\mathcal{J}_{0}
, write
V=U\oplus U^{\perp}
and let
B(i)
be induced by the monoid homomorphism sending
(t,\phi)\in\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times U)
to
(t,\phi\oplus\mathrm{Id}_{U^{\perp}})\in\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times U\oplus U^{\perp})
. Then

\Phi^{B}_{\infty}:\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{\infty})/\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M)\longrightarrow\Omega^{\infty}(\Theta B^{(1)})

should be
2
2

2

Though
\Phi^{B}_{\infty}
is not visibly the same map as the one appearing in loc. cit., they share the same formal properties by Proposition
2.2
.
the map from
[
WW88
, Thm. C]
, and Proposition
2.2
recovers
[
WW88
, Prop. 3.1]
in this case.

Notation 2.6
.

In the remainder of Section
2
, we will denote by
E(-)
and
B(-)
the orthogonal functors given on objects by

(2.9)

E(V):=B\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}((M-\nu P)\times V),\qquad B(V):=B\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times V),

where
\nu P
is an open tubular neighbourhood of the embedding
\iota:P\subset M
, and on morphisms as in Example
2.5
. There is a natural transformation
E(-)\to B(-)
given by extending a diffeomorphism by the identity on
\nu P\times(-)
, and the orthogonal functor
F(-):=\operatorname{hofib}(E(-)\to B(-))
will play a especially important role in the proof of Theorem
A
. We will often use the following notation for the derivatives of these functors:

(2.10)

\mathbf{CE}(P,M):=\Theta F^{(1)},\qquad\mathbf{H}(M-\nu P):=\Theta E^{(1)},\qquad\mathbf{H}(M):=\Theta B^{(1)}.

Note that
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
is, by definition, the homotopy fibre of the natural map
\mathbf{H}(M-\nu P)\to\mathbf{H}(M)
.

Remark 2.7
.

Let us comment on the notation in (
2.10
). Write
H(M)
for the space of smooth
h
-cobordisms starting at
M
(cf. Section
5.2
). We will see in Remark
3.10
that there is a natural equivalence (of spaces)

(2.11)

\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{H}(M)\simeq\mathcal{H}(M):=\underset{k}{\hocolim}\ H(M\times D^{k}),

where the colimit on the right hand side—the
stable
h
-cobordism space
—is induced by the
h
-cobordism stabilisation maps of Appendix
B
. By the stable parametrised
h
-cobordism theorem of Waldhausen–Jahren–Rognes
[
WJR13a
]
, the infinite loop space of the desuspension of the
smooth Whitehead spectrum

\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)
is also equivalent to
\mathcal{H}(M)
(as ordinary spaces). Moreover, Weiss and Williams showed in
[
WW88
, Cor. 5.6]
that the spectra
\Theta B^{(1)}=\mathbf{H}(M)
and
\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M)
also share the same negative homotopy groups, which led them to rename the former as the latter. This, though conjecturally true, was not fully justified since no equivalence between these two spectra was given.

Note also that the
O(1)
-spectrum
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)=\Theta F^{(1)}
is, by definition, the homotopy fibre of the map
\mathbf{H}(M-\nu P)\to\mathbf{H}(M)
. We hope that the homotopy fibre sequence
C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M)\to H(M-\nu P)\to H(M)
and Proposition
3.11
together explain this choice of notation.

The spaces of bounded diffeomorphisms are usually defined as the geometric realisations of certain simplicial group/set. Before we recall these simplicial models in Definition
2.8
below, let us fix some notation first. For a subset
S\subset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{p+1}
and
\epsilon>0
, let
B_{\epsilon}(S)\subset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{p+1}
denote the open
\epsilon
-ball around
S
. For any face
\sigma\subset\Delta^{p}\subset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{p+1}
and
0<\epsilon<1/2
, fix identifications
B_{\epsilon}(\partial\sigma)\cap\sigma\cong\partial\sigma\times[0,\times\epsilon)
which are radial with respect to the barycenter for
\sigma
. We will say that a continuous map
f:X\times\Delta^{p}\to Y\times\Delta^{p}
over
\Delta^{p}
(i.e.
\mathrm{proj}_{\Delta^{p}}=\mathrm{proj}_{\Delta^{p}}\circ f
) satisfies the
\epsilon
-collaring condition
if for every face
\sigma\subset\Delta^{p}
,

f\mid_{X\times(B_{\epsilon}(\sigma)\cap\sigma)}\equiv f\mid_{X\times\partial\sigma}\times\mathrm{Id}_{[0,\epsilon)}.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
V\in\mathcal{J}
. The
simplicial group

\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}^{b}(M\times V)_{\bullet}

of bounded diffeomorphisms
of
M\times V
relative to
\partial M\times V
has as
p
-simplices the set of diffeomorphisms of
\Delta^{p}\times M\times V
over
\Delta^{p}
which are bounded (with respect to
V
), that are the identity in a neighbourhood of
\Delta^{p}\times\partial M\times V
, and that satisfy the
\epsilon
-collaring condition for some
0<\epsilon<1/2
. Face and degeneracy maps are determined by the coface and codegeneracy maps of the cosimplicial space
\Delta^{\bullet}
. If we relax the condition on diffeomorphisms to be over
\Delta^{p}
to only face-preserving (i.e. diffeomorphisms that send
\sigma\times M\times V
to itself for every face
\sigma\subset\Delta^{p}
), we obtain the simplicial group
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}{\vphantom{\mathrm{Diff}}}^{\hskip 1.0ptb}_{\partial}(M\times V)_{\bullet}
of
bounded block diffeomorphisms
of
M\times V
.

Warning 2.9
.

One could have defined the orthogonal functor
B(-)
of Notation
2.6
, for instance, to be

\mathcal{J}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathsf{Top}_{*},\quad U\longmapsto B|\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}^{b}(M\times U)_{\bullet}|.

This latter rule, however, does not give rise to a continuous functor in the sense of orthogonal calculus, i.e., it is not enriched over
\mathsf{Top}_{*}
. A way to fix this is to replace
\mathsf{Top}_{*}
by
\mathsf{sSet}_{*}
,
\mathcal{J}_{0}
by a category
\mathcal{J}_{0}^{\Delta}
enriched now over
\mathsf{sSet}_{*}
, and doing orthogonal calculus to
\mathsf{sSet}_{*}
-enriched functors
\mathcal{J}_{0}^{\Delta}\to\mathsf{sSet}_{*}
. This is morally the point of view taken by Weiss and Williams in
[
WW88
]
, but orthogonal calculus for simplicially enriched functors has not yet been carried out rigorously, so we prefer to not pursue this approach.

The simplicial models of Definition
2.8
are more convenient to work with than the point-set topological ones of Definition
2.4
. Moreover, we will need some results of
[
WW88
]
that are stated in the simplicial setting, and so we will have to argue that both models share the same weak homotopy type.

Proposition
A.1
.

There is a zig-zag of weak equivalences of simplicial group-like monoids

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times V)_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Sing}_{\bullet}(\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}^{b}(M\times V)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

In particular, there is a zig-zag of weak equivalences of group-like topological monoids connecting
|\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times V)_{\bullet}|
and
\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}^{b}(M\times V)
.

We defer the proof of this proposition to Section
A.1
in the appendix.

3.
Proof of Theorem
A

We now prove Theorem
A
. Section
3.1
will first reduce it to the case when
P
is a codimension zero submanifold of
M
. Some necessary preliminaries will be presented in Section
3.2
. Finally the map
\Phi^{\operatorname{Emb}}
of Theorem
A
and its connectivity will be analysed in Sections
3.3
and
3.4
.

Before we move on to the next section, let us record a disjunction result for concordance embeddings known as
Hudson’s concordance-implies-isotopy theorem

[
Hud70
, Thm. 2.1, Addendum 2.1.2]
.

Theorem 3.1

(Hudson)
.

The space
C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M)
is connected if
p\leq d-3
. Equivalently, the natural map
\pi_{0}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M))\to\pi_{0}(\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M))
is an isomorphism.

Remark 3.2
.

Hudson’s theorem also holds in the piecewise linear setting (cf.
[
Hud70
, Thm. 1.5]
).

3.1.
Reduction to geometric codimension zero embeddings

Let
\iota:P\xhookrightarrow{}M
be as in the statement of Theorem
A
. It will be convenient to be able to assume that
P\subset M
is a codimension zero submanifold (though of handle codimension at least
3
). The following result deals with this technicality, and shows that the difference between block and ordinary
smooth
embeddings is insensitive to the geometric codimension.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
M^{d}
be a compact smooth Riemannian manifold and
\iota:P^{p}\xhookrightarrow{}M^{d}
a neat submanifold that is closed as a subspace. Let
\overline{\nu}P
be the closed disk bundle of the normal bundle
\nu_{\iota}
of the embedding
\iota
, and let
\hat{\iota}:\overline{\nu}P\xhookrightarrow{}M
be the induced embedding. Then the square

(3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\hat{\iota}}(\overline{\nu}P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\iota}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\hat{\iota}}(\overline{\nu}P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\iota}(P,M).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{res}_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathrm{res}}_{P}}

is homotopy cartesian. Here the subscripts
\iota
or
\hat{\iota}
in the embedding spaces stand for the path component consisting of embeddings isotopic to
\iota
or
\hat{\iota}
(relative to
\partial_{0}P
).

Equivalently, by taking vertical homotopy fibres in (
3.1
) and noting Hudson’s Theorem
3.1
and that
\mathrm{res}_{P}
and
\widetilde{\mathrm{res}}_{P}
are surjective, there is a weak equivalence

\operatorname{hofib}_{\iota}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)\xhookrightarrow{}\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M))\simeq\operatorname{hofib}_{\hat{\iota}}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(\overline{\nu}P,M)\xhookrightarrow{}\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(\overline{\nu}P,M)).

Proof.

We will show that the horizontal homotopy fibre of the vertical inclusions in (
3.1
) can be identified, up to equivalence, with the identity map of the topological group
\mathrm{Aut}_{\partial_{0}}(\nu_{\iota})
of bundle automorphisms of
\nu_{\iota}
which are standard near
\partial_{0}P
. In particular, the total homotopy fibre of (
3.1
) will be weakly contractible.

We first deal with the top horizontal homotopy fibre. Consider the fibration

E:=\left\{\hbox to82.87pt{\vbox to49.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 41.4347pt\lower-22.6389pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-41.4347pt}{-18.97917pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.58768pt}{-12.76532pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;\middle|\;\begin{aligned} &\varphi\in\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\iota}(P,M),\\
&G\in\operatorname{BunInj}_{\partial_{0}}(\nu_{\iota},\tau_{M}),\\
&D\varphi\oplus G:\tau_{P}\oplus\nu_{\iota}\cong\varphi^{*}\tau_{M}.\end{aligned}\right\}\overset{r}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\iota}(P,M),\quad(G,\varphi)\longmapsto\varphi.

Taking derivatives at the zero section of
\overline{\nu}P
defines a map
D:\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\hat{\iota}}(\overline{\nu}P,M)\to E
over
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\iota}(P,M)
. A homotopy inverse
E\to\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\hat{\iota}}(\overline{\nu}P,M)
to
D
can be defined using the exponential map. Therefore the homotopy fibre of
\mathrm{res}_{P}
is equivalent to the fibre of
r
(observe that
r
is a fibration). Now
\iota^{*}\tau_{M}
is already identified with
\tau_{P}\oplus\nu_{\iota}
, so the fibre
F:=r^{-1}(\iota)
can be described as the subspace of bundle automorphisms of
\tau_{P}\oplus\nu_{\iota}
over
P
which are the identity on the tangent summand
\tau_{P}
(and near
\partial_{0}P
). As the space of bundle maps
\nu_{\iota}\to\tau_{P}
over
P
is contractible, it follows that the inclusion
\mathrm{Aut}_{\partial_{0}}(\nu_{\iota})\xhookrightarrow{}F
is a homotopy equivalence.

The argument for the bottom map of (
3.1
) is similar but trickier; we work with the simplicial model of block embeddings of Definition
2.8
. First let
\xi
and
\pi
be vector bundles over spaces
B
and
B^{\prime}
, respectively, and fix some bundle map
I:\xi\to\pi
. For any closed subset
\partial_{0}\subset B
, let
\operatorname{\widetilde{BunMap}}_{\partial_{0}}(\xi,\pi)_{\bullet}
denote the semi-simplicial set whose
n
-simplices consist of bundle maps
G:\Delta^{n}\times\xi\to\tau_{\Delta^{n}}\boxplus\pi:=(\tau_{\Delta^{n}}\times B^{\prime})\oplus(\Delta^{n}\times\pi)
such that

•

G
agrees with
\mathbf{0}_{\Delta^{n}}\boxplus I
near
\Delta^{n}\times\partial_{0}
, where
\mathbf{0}_{\Delta^{n}}:\epsilon^{0}_{\Delta^{n}}\cong\Delta^{n}\to\tau_{\Delta^{n}}
is the inclusion as the zero section, and

•

for every face
\sigma\subset\Delta^{n}
,
G(\sigma\times\xi)\subset\tau_{\sigma}\boxplus\pi\subset\tau_{\Delta^{n}}\boxplus\pi
.

Given a map
i:B\to B^{\prime}
which agrees with the underlying map of
I
on
\partial_{0}\subset B
, let
\operatorname{\widetilde{BunMap}}_{\partial_{0}}(\xi,\pi;i)_{\bullet}
the semi-simplicial subset consisting of those bundle maps
G
whose underlying map on the base spaces
\Delta^{n}\times B\to\Delta^{n}\times B^{\prime}
is
\mathrm{Id}_{\Delta^{n}}\times i
. Let
\operatorname{\widetilde{BunInj}}_{\partial_{0}}(\xi,\pi)_{\bullet}
and
\operatorname{\widetilde{BunInj}}_{\partial_{0}}(\xi,\pi;i)_{\bullet}
be the semi-simplicial subsets of those bundle maps that are fibrewise injective. Then again, taking derivatives at the zero section of
\Delta^{\bullet}\times\overline{\nu}P
yields a simplicial map
\widetilde{D}_{\bullet}
from
\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\hat{\iota}}(\overline{\nu}P,M)_{\bullet}
to a semi-simplicial set
\widetilde{E}_{\bullet}
whose
n
-simplices are

\widetilde{E}_{n}:=\left\{\hbox to132.99pt{\vbox to50.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 66.4955pt\lower-24.43251pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
