{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.22977pt}{-13.72562pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\;\middle|\;\begin{aligned} &\varphi\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\iota}(P,M)_{n},\\
&G\in\operatorname{\widetilde{BunInj}}_{\partial_{0}}(\nu_{\iota},\tau_{M})_{n},\\
D\varphi\oplus G&:\tau_{\Delta^{n}}\boxplus(\tau_{P}\oplus\nu_{\iota})\cong\varphi^{*}(\tau_{\Delta^{n}}\boxplus\tau_{M}).\end{aligned}\right\},

and whose face maps are given by restriction to face strata. The map
\widetilde{r}_{\bullet}:\widetilde{E}_{\bullet}\to\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)_{\bullet}
given by
\widetilde{r}(G,\varphi):=\varphi
is now a Kan fibration, and
\widetilde{r}_{\bullet}\circ\widetilde{D}_{\bullet}=\widetilde{\mathrm{res}}_{P}
. By a similar argument as in the previous case, the homotopy fibre of
\widetilde{\mathrm{res}}_{P}
is equivalent to the fibre of
\widetilde{r}_{\bullet}
. Using the canonical identification

(\mathrm{Id}_{\Delta^{n}}\times\iota)^{*}(\tau_{\Delta^{n}}\boxplus\tau_{M})=\tau_{\Delta^{n}}\boxplus\iota^{*}\tau_{M}\cong\tau_{\Delta^{n}}\boxplus(\tau_{P}\oplus\nu_{\iota}),

the fibre
\widetilde{F}_{\bullet}:=\widetilde{r}^{-1}_{\bullet}(\iota)
is isomorphic to the semi-simplicial subset of
\operatorname{\widetilde{BunInj}}_{\partial_{0}}(\nu_{\iota},\iota^{*}\tau_{M};\mathrm{Id}_{P})_{\bullet}
of bundle maps

G=G_{\Delta^{n}}\oplus G_{\tau}\oplus G_{\nu}:\Delta^{n}\times\nu_{\iota}\longrightarrow\tau_{\Delta^{n}}\boxplus(\tau_{P}\oplus\nu_{\iota})=(\tau_{\Delta^{n}}\times P)\oplus(\Delta^{n}\times\tau_{P})\oplus(\Delta^{n}\times\nu_{\iota})

for which
G_{\nu}
is an isomorphism. Thus it follows that

\widetilde{F}_{\bullet}=\operatorname{\widetilde{BunMap}}_{\partial_{0}}(\nu_{\iota},\tau_{P};\mathrm{Id}_{P})_{\bullet}\times\operatorname{Aut}_{\partial_{0}}(\nu_{\iota})_{\bullet},

where the boundary condition on
\operatorname{\widetilde{BunMap}}_{\partial_{0}}(\nu_{\iota},\tau_{P};\mathrm{Id}_{P})_{\bullet}
forces bundle maps to be zero near
\Delta^{\bullet}\times\partial_{0}P
. Clearly
\operatorname{\widetilde{BunMap}}_{\partial_{0}}(\nu_{\iota},\tau_{P};\mathrm{Id}_{P})_{\bullet}
is weakly contractible: indeed given an
n
-cycle
G
in this semi-simplicial set, a nullhomotopy of
G
is roughly given by regarding
\Delta^{n+1}
as
(\Delta^{n}\times[0,1],\Delta^{n}\times\{0\})
and applying
t\cdot G
on
\Delta^{n}\times\{t\}
, for
0\leq t\leq 1
. Therefore
|\widetilde{F}_{\bullet}|\simeq|\mathrm{Aut}_{\partial_{0}}(\nu_{\iota})_{\bullet}|=\mathrm{Aut}_{\partial_{0}}(\nu_{\iota})
, as required.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

Proposition
3.3
is false in the topological setting. First of all, a locally flat embedding
\iota:P^{p}\xhookrightarrow{}M^{d}
does not always admit a normal microbundle (cf.
[
RS67
]
; they do admit one stably though
[
Hir66
, Thm. B]
). But even if it did, the statement would still not hold in general: consider the case
(M,P)=(D^{d},D^{p})
for
p\leq d-3
. Then both
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}^{\mathrm{Top}}(D^{p},D^{d})
and
\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}^{\mathrm{Top}}(D^{p},D^{d})
are contractible by the Alexander trick. However, using the topological version of Theorem
A
(cf. Remark
1.3
), we will see in Remark
6.3
that the homotopy fibre of the map

\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{Top}}(D^{p}\times D^{d-p},D^{d})\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{Top}}(D^{d}\times D^{d-p},D^{d})

is not contractible. In particular, the topological analogue of the square (
3.1
) cannot possibly be homotopy cartesian in this case.

3.2.
Last ingredients

From now on, let
\iota:P^{d}\xhookrightarrow{}M^{d}
be a codimension zero closed embedding that meets
\partial M
transversally in
\partial_{0}P
, and denote by
p
the handle dimension of
P
relative to
\partial_{0}P
; we will write
\overline{M-P}
instead of the isotopy equivalent manifold
M-\nu P
to emphasise that
P
has codimension zero in
M
. It suffices to prove Theorem
A
in this case by Proposition
3.3
. We now present the last necessary prelimary results.

3.2.1.
Parametrised isotopy extension theorem

The
parametrised isotopy extension theorem
states that for
\varphi_{t}:P\xhookrightarrow{}M
any continuous family of embeddings parametrised by
t\in\Delta^{k}
(with
P

compact
), there exists a continuous family of diffeomorphisms
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in\Delta^{k}}
of
M
(which are the identity away from a compact set of
M
) such that
\phi_{0}=\mathrm{Id}_{M}
and
\phi_{t}(\varphi_{0}(x))=\varphi_{t}(x)
for all
(x,t)\in P\times\Delta^{k}
. Moreover, if
K\subset\Delta^{k}
is some contractible subcomplex containing the
0
-th vertex and
\{\phi^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in K}
is another continuous family of diffeomorphisms of
M
parametrised by
K
such that
\phi^{\prime}_{0}=\mathrm{Id}_{M}
and
\phi^{\prime}_{t}(\varphi_{0}(x))=\varphi_{t}(x)
for all
(x,t)\in P\times K
, then we can arrange
\{\phi_{t}\}_{t\in\Delta^{k}}
as above to agree with
\{\phi^{\prime}_{t}\}_{t\in K}
on
K
. A consequence of this fact due to Palais
[
Pal60
]
(see
[
Lim64
]
for a simple proof) is that the restriction map
\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M)\to\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)
is a locally trivial fibre bundle with
\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(\overline{M-P})
as fibre. Such fibration can be delooped to the homotopy fibre sequence

(3.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(\overline{M-P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M),}

where the subscript
\langle\iota\rangle
stands for the union of all the components in
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)
that contain embeddings of the form
\phi\circ\iota
for
\phi\in\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}(M)
. By replacing
P
and
M
in (
3.2
) by
P\times I
and
M\times I
, and modifying the boundary conditions, we get a similar homotopy fibre sequence

(3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC(\overline{M-P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces BC(M).}

Note that
C\mathrm{Emb}(M,P)
is connected by Hudson’s Theorem
3.1
. Finally, there is a block analogue of (
3.2
).

Proposition 3.5
.

There is a homotopy fibre sequence

(3.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(\overline{M-P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M).}

Proof.

There is a right action of the simplicial group
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(\overline{M-P})_{\bullet}
on
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)_{\bullet}
; we will write
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)_{\bullet}/\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(\overline{M-P})_{\bullet}
for the simplicial set of (level-wise) cosets of this right action. The geometric realisation
|\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)_{\bullet}/\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(\overline{M-P})_{\bullet}|
of this simplicial set is homotopy equivalent to the homotopy fibre of the right map of (
3.4
), so it suffices to show that the action map

a:\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)_{\bullet}/\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(\overline{M-P})_{\bullet}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M)_{\bullet},\quad[\phi]\longmapsto\phi\circ\iota

is an isomorphism. It is visibly injective, for if
\phi\circ\iota=\psi\circ\iota
for
\phi,\psi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)_{\bullet}
, then
\psi^{-1}\circ\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(\overline{M-P})_{\bullet}
and hence
[\psi]=[\psi\circ\psi^{-1}\circ\phi]=[\phi]
in
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)_{\bullet}/\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(\overline{M-P})_{\bullet}
.

For surjectivity, let
\varphi
be some
k
-simplex in
\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M)_{\bullet}
. Then there exists some
\phi\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)_{k}
for which
\varphi
and
\phi\circ\iota
lie in the same component in
\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)_{\bullet}
. Then
\varphi^{\prime}:=\phi^{-1}\circ\varphi\in\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\iota}(P,M)_{k}
and, in fact, we can arrange that its restriction to the zero-th vertex
\varphi^{\prime}_{0}
is
\iota
by rechoosing
\phi
(if necessary) using the isotopy extension theorem. Then applying the isotopy extension theorem to
\varphi^{\prime}
restricted to each of the faces that contains the
0
-th vertex, inductively on the dimension of the face, we obtain some
\Phi^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)_{k}
such that
\Phi^{\prime}\mid_{P\times\Delta^{k}}\equiv\varphi^{\prime}
. Then
\Phi:=\phi\circ\Phi^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(M)_{k}
is such that
\Phi\mid_{P\times\Delta^{k}}\equiv\varphi
, as desired.
∎

Remark 3.6
.

There also exists a topological version of the isotopy extension theorem
[
EK71
, Cor. 1.4]
. The same proof as above also works in the topological setting.

Remark 3.7

(Speculative)
.

Weiss and Williams point out in
[
WW88
, §1]
that an analogue of the (parametrised) isotopy extension theorem in the bounded setting does not hold (see
[
Hir76
, Ch. 8, Ex. 9]
for a counterexample in codimension
2
). However, we believe that a weaker version of the theorem should still hold: namely, for
V\in\mathcal{J}_{0}
define the bounded embedding space
\operatorname{Emb}^{b}_{\partial_{0}}(P\times V,M\times V)
as in Definition
2.4
. Then, there should be a homotopy fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}^{b}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P\times V,M\times V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(V)=B\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}^{b}(\overline{M-P}\times V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(V)=B\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}^{b}(M\times V),}

where
E(-)
and
B(-)
are as in Notation
2.6
. We will not give a proof of this claim, as it seems rather technical and we will not need it for the argument of Theorem
A
. The reader may however find it useful to think of the orthogonal functor
F(-):=\operatorname{hofib}(E(-)\to B(-))
as
\operatorname{Emb}^{b}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P\times(-),M\times(-))
.

3.2.2.
Alexander trick-like equivalences

For
V\in\mathcal{J}_{0}
, let
D(V)\subset V
denote the correspoding closed unit disk (so that
D^{k}=D({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{k})
). The following is proved in Propositions 1.8, 1.10 and 1.12 of
[
WW88
]
. Even though we state it for the orthogonal functor
B(-)
of Notation
2.6
, it of course holds for
E(-)
too.

Proposition 3.8
.

For
V\in\mathcal{J}_{0}
, the Alexander trick-like map

\mathrm{alex}:C(M\times D(V))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Omega^{V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}}B^{(1)}(V)=\Omega^{V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}}\left(\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}})/\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times V)\right)

is a weak equivalence. Moreover, there is a homotopy commutative diagram

(3.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(M\times D(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(M\times D(V)\times D^{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C(M\times D(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}}B^{(1)}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{2}}B^{(1)}(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\vee}_{V}}

where
\Sigma
denotes the usual concordance stabilisation map and
s^{\vee}_{V}
is the adjoint of the structure map (
2.2
) for the orthogonal spectrum
\Theta B^{(1)}=\mathbf{H}(M)
.

Remark 3.9
.

Both the domain and codomain of the map ”alex” of Proposition
3.8
are group-like
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-spaces; the former by composition of concordance diffeomorphisms, and the latter by the loop space structure induced by
\Omega^{{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}}(-)
. In Section
5.2
, we construct a (non-connected) delooping of this map (see (
5.4
)).

It seems likely that the homotopy commutative square (
3.5
) can also be delooped in a similar manner. Proving this, however, is quite technical and we will not need it in any case. What we will need instead is the observation that if
M
is replaced by
M\times I
in Proposition
3.8
, the whole statement can be delooped once with respect to the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-structures induced by
stacking in the
I
-direction
. This is straightforward to check from the proofs in loc. cit.

Remark 3.10
.

It follows from this proposition that there is a natural
3
3

3

Natural in codimension zero embeddings.
equivalence

\Omega^{\infty+1}\mathbf{H}(M)\simeq\mathcal{C}(M):=\underset{k}{\hocolim}\ C(M\times D^{k}).

As pointed out in Remark
3.9
, this equivalence can be delooped once if we replace
M
by
M\times I
. Moreover, the (non-equivariant) homotopy types of both
\mathbf{H}(-)
and
\mathcal{C}(-)
are invariant under crossing with
I
, namely, there are natural equivalences
\mathbf{H}(M\times I)\simeq\mathbf{H}(M)
(by Lemma
5.13
below) and
\mathcal{C}(M\times I)\simeq\mathcal{C}(M)
(by definition). By this line of reasoning, we obtain natural equivalences

\Omega^{\infty}_{0}\mathbf{H}(M)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}_{0}\mathbf{H}(M\times I)\simeq B\mathcal{C}(M\times I)\simeq B\mathcal{C}(M)

By
[
Vog85
, Prop. 2.1]
,
B\mathcal{C}(M)
is also naturally equivalent to the basepoint component of the space of stable
h
-cobordisms
\mathcal{H}(M)
of Remark
2.7
, and by
[
WW88
, Cor. 5.6]
and the
s
-cobordism theorem, the groups
\pi_{0}^{s}(\mathbf{H}(M))
and
\pi_{0}(\mathcal{H}(M))
are both isomorphic to the Whitehead group
\mathrm{Wh}(\pi_{1}M)
. Since there is an obvious equivalence
of spaces

\Omega^{\infty}X\simeq\Omega^{\infty}_{0}X\times\pi_{0}^{s}(X)
, we obtain the promised natural equivalence (
2.11
)

\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{H}(M)\simeq\mathcal{H}(M).

This, of course, ought to be an equivalence of infinite loop spaces, but that seems to be more difficult to see.

Proof of Proposition
3.8
.

This is proved in Propositions 1.8, 1.10 and Lemma 1.12 of
[
WW88
]
. Let us just explain how the Alexander trick-like map

\mathrm{alex}:C(M\times D(V))\longrightarrow\Omega^{V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}}\left(\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}})/\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times V)\right)

is defined: given a concordance diffeomorphism
\phi:M\times D(V)\times I\cong M\times D(V)\times I
, extend it by
\phi\mid_{M\times D(V)\times\{1\}}\times\mathrm{Id}_{[1,+\infty)}
on
M\times D(V)\times[1,+\infty)
and by the identity elsewhere to obtain a bounded self-diffeomorphism
\widehat{\phi}
of
M\times V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}
; then shift it along
V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}
to obtain a
(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}})
-fold loop in
\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}})/\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times V)
. We refer to loc. cit. for the rest of the proofs.
∎

Taking fibres of Proposition
3.8
for
E(-)
and
B(-)
yields the first part of the analogous result for
F(-)
.

Proposition 3.11
.

For
V\in\mathcal{J}_{0}
, there are weak equivalences

\mathrm{alex}:\Omega C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times D(V),M\times D(V))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Omega^{1+V}F^{(1)}(V),

making the following diagram commute up to homotopy:

(3.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times D(V),M\times D(V))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times D(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}),M\times D(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1+V}F^{(1)}(V)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{1+V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}}F^{(1)}(V\oplus{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\vee}_{V}}

where
\Sigma
here is the concordance embedding stabilisation map of Section
1.1.1
. Moreover,

(3.7)

\Omega^{\infty}(\mathbf{CE}(P,M)):=\Omega^{\infty}(\Theta F^{(1)})\simeq\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P,M):=\underset{k}{\hocolim}\ C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times D^{k},M\times D^{k}).

To establish (
3.7
), we will need the following result, which was suggested to us by Manuel Krannich.

Lemma 3.12
.

For
p\leq d-3
and
n\geq 0
, the space
\Theta F^{(1)}_{n}=F^{(1)}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{n})
is
n
-connected.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the natural map
\Theta E^{(1)}_{n}\to\Theta B^{(1)}_{n}
, call it
\lambda
, is such that
\pi_{*}(\lambda)
is

(
a
) surjective if
*=n+1
,

(
b
) injective if
*=0
,

(
c
) an isomorphism if
1\leq*\leq n
.

For (
a
), observe that the map
\Omega^{n+1}\lambda
is, up to equivalence, the natural map of concordance spaces
C(\overline{M-P}\times D^{n})\to C(M\times D^{n})
by Proposition
3.8
. Then by exactness of

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(C(\overline{M-P}\times D^{n}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(C(M\times D^{n}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}(C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times D^{n},M\times D^{n}))=*,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{n+1}(\lambda)}

where the equality on the right is the statement of Hudson’s Theorem
3.1
, it follows that
\pi_{n+1}(\lambda)
is surjective.

For
(b)
and
(c)
, consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta E^{(1)}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta B^{(1)}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(\Sigma^{n}\Theta E^{(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(\Sigma^{n}\Theta B^{(1)}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{stab.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{stab.}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

We claim that the map of (non-connective) spectra
\mathbf{H}(\overline{M-P})\to\mathbf{H}(M)
underlying
\eta
is an isomorphism in
\pi_{*}^{s}
for
*\leq 0
: indeed, the inclusion
\overline{M-P}\xhookrightarrow{}M
is
2
-connected and
\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbf{H}(-))
for
*\leq 0
(see (
3.9
)) only depends on
\pi_{1}(-)
by
[
WW88
, Cor. 5.6]
. So
\eta
itself satisfies (
b
) and (
c
). Now by
[
WW88
, Cor. 5.8]
, both vertical maps are injective in
\pi_{0}
and isomorphisms in
\pi_{1\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}*\leq n}
. Claims
(b)
and
(c)
now follow.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.11
.

It remains to deloop the natural equivalence
\Omega^{\infty+1}(\mathbf{CE}(P,M))\simeq\Omega\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P,M)
obtained from the squares (
3.6
), so to yield (
3.7
). We do this as in Remark
3.10
.

First observe that both
\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
and
\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P,M)
are connected—the former by Lemma
3.12
and the latter by Hudson’s Theorem
3.1
. Just like in Remark
3.9
, the homotopy commutative square (
3.6
) can be delooped if we replace
\iota:P\xhookrightarrow{}M
by
\iota\times\mathrm{Id}_{I}:P\times I\xhookrightarrow{}M\times I
. Finally, observe that there are natural equivalences
\mathbf{CE}(P\times I,M\times I)\simeq\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
(by Lemma
5.13
) and
\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P\times I,M\times I)\simeq\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P,M)
(by definition). We thus obtain the desired chain of natural equivalences

\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{CE}(P,M)\simeq\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{CE}(P\times I,M\times I)\simeq\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P\times I,M\times I)\simeq\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P,M).

∎

3.3.
The map
\Phi^{\operatorname{Emb}}
of Theorem
A

Recall the map
\Phi^{F}_{\infty}
of Proposition
2.6
for
F(-)
. Noting that
F(0)\simeq\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M)
by the isotopy extension sequence (
3.2
), this map, up to equivalence, takes the form

\Phi^{F}_{\infty}:\operatorname{hofib}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M)\to F({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{\infty}))\longrightarrow\Omega^{\infty}(\mathbf{CE}(P,M)_{hC_{2}}).

To obtain
\Phi^{\operatorname{Emb}}
, we need to replace
F({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{\infty})
by
\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M)
above (and deal with some path-components considerations). This turns out to be possible by a principle similar to that of
[
WW88
, Rem. 3.5]
.

Proposition 3.13
.

If
p\leq d-3
, then there is a (zig-zag of) weak equivalence

\operatorname{hofib}_{\iota}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)\to\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M))\simeq\operatorname{hofib}(F(0)\to F({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{\infty})).

Proof.

First observe that because
C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M)
is connected (by Hudson’s Theorem
3.1
), we have that

\displaystyle\operatorname{hofib}_{\iota}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)\xhookrightarrow{}\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{hofib}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\iota}(P,M)\xhookrightarrow{}\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\iota}(P,M))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{hofib}_{\iota}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M)\xhookrightarrow{}\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M)).

Therefore, it suffices to construct a homotopy commutative diagram

(3.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{F}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{\infty})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0},\langle\iota\rangle}(P,M).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

\scriptstyle\sim

For a group

. More explicitly,

\kappa_{j}(\pi)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{Wh}_{1}(\pi),&j=1,\\
\widetilde{K}_{0}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}\pi),&j=0,\\

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}(\widetilde{B}^{(1)}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{n}))}

3.4.
Connectivity of the map
\Phi^{\operatorname{Emb}}

In this section we show that the map
\Phi^{\operatorname{Emb}}
just defined is
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)
-connected, at last establishing Theorem
A
(modulo the proof of Proposition
A.1
). The connectivity of
\Phi^{\operatorname{Emb}}
is that of
\Phi^{F}_{\infty}
; we show by induction on
n\geq 0
that the maps
\Phi^{F}_{n}
of Proposition
2.2
are at least
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)
-connected. Note that this is clear for
n=0
, as both the domain and codomain are contractible.

Suppose now that
\Phi^{F}_{n}
is
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)
-connected for some
n\geq 0
. To show that
\Phi^{F}_{n+1}
has this connectivity, it suffices to show that the map
\mathrm{stab.}:\Theta F^{(1)}_{n}\to\Omega^{\infty}(\Sigma^{n}\Theta F^{(1)})
of Proposition
2.2
is
(\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)+n)
-connected. But
\Theta F^{(1)}_{n}
is
n
-connected by Lemma
3.12
and
\Sigma^{n}\Theta F^{(1)}
is
(n+1)
-connective. So it suffices to show that
\Omega^{n+1}(\mathrm{stab.})
is
(\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)-1)
-connected. This follows from the homotopy commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times D^{n},M\times D^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{n+1}\Theta F^{(1)}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty+1}(\Theta F^{(1)}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ref{firstderivativeF})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{n+1}(\mathrm{stab.})}

By definition, the connectivity of the top horizontal map is
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d+n,p)-1\geq\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)-1
. One obtains the above homotopy commutative diagram from stacking together squares of the form (
3.6
) with
V={\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{k}
for
k\geq n
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
A
.∎

4.
A splitting result for embedding spaces

In this section we derive, as a consequence of Theorem
A
, a general splitting result
4
4

4

This should be compared to the analogous result of Burghelea and Lashof
[
BL82
, Cor. E]
.
for embedding spaces of manifolds with interval factors. This will then be used for the splitting part of Theorem
B
. All throughout, let
\iota:P^{p}\subset M^{d}
be as in the statement of Theorem
A
.

For
D(-)
any of
\mathrm{Diff}_{\partial}^{b}(-\times V)
with
V\in\mathcal{J}_{0}
,
\widetilde{\mathrm{Diff}}_{\partial}(-)
or
C(-)
, there are
graphing maps

\Gamma:\Omega D(M)\longrightarrow D(M\times I)

given (roughly) by regarding a
1
-parameter family of automorphisms of
M
as an automorphism of
M\times I
itself. These are natural with respect to codimension zero embeddings. Moreover, these maps can be delooped as, up to homotopy, they intertwine the (group-like)
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-structures of concatenating loops for the domain, and stacking automorphisms in the
I
-direction for the codomain. There are similar maps

(4.1)

\Gamma:\Omega E(P,M)\longrightarrow E(P\times I,M\times I)

for
E(-,-)
any of
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(-,-)
,
\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(-,-)
,
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}^{(\sim)}(-,-)
(see (
1.4
)) or
C\mathrm{Emb}(-,-)
. In what follows, we will write
\Gamma
for any map of this same nature.

Remark 4.1
.

Most of the functors
D(-)
and
E(-,-)
above either admit a point-set topological model or a simplicial model. In the first case, the graphing maps just introduced are really zig-zags of maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega E(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\mathsf{col},\mathsf{sm}}E(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E(P\times I,M\times I),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}

where, for
X
a pointed (Fréchet) manifold, here
\Omega^{\mathsf{col},\mathsf{sm}}X
stands for the space of smooth loops
\gamma:S^{1}\to X
which are
collared
in the sense that there exists some neighbourhood of
1\in S^{1}
which is sent by
\gamma
to the basepoint in
X
. The inclusion
\Omega^{\mathsf{col},\mathsf{sm}}E(P,M)\xhookrightarrow{}\Omega E(P,M)
is an equivalence by smooth approximation of continuous functions.

In the simplicial case, the graphing maps
\Gamma
are the geometric realisations of the simplicial maps

\Gamma_{\bullet}:(\Omega E(P,M))_{\bullet}\longrightarrow E(P\times I,M\times I)_{\bullet}

that send a
q
-simplex in
(\Omega E(P,M))_{\bullet}
(seen as a
(q+1)
-simplex
g\in E(P,M)_{\bullet}
whose
0
-th face and vertex are the basepoint
*\in E(P,M)_{\bullet}
) to the
q
-simplex in
E(P\times I,M\times I)_{\bullet}
obtained from
g
by expanding out the
0
-th vertex of
\Delta^{q+1}
to a
q
-dimensional simplex (i.e. regarding
\Delta^{q+1}
as
(\Delta^{q}\times I,\Delta^{q}\times\{0\})
).

In the cases when
D(-)
or
E(-,-)
admit both models, one verifies that these two graphing maps agree up to homotopy. We ignore both of these technicalities in most of what follows.

Theorem 4.2
.

Let
I
and
J
both denote closed intervals. For
p\leq d-3
,
N:=\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d+1,p+1)-1
and
N^{\prime}:=\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d+2,p+2)-1
, there are equivalences away from
2

\displaystyle\Omega\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N}\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P\times I,M\times I)\simeq_{[\frac{1}{2}]}\Omega\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N}\left(\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P\times I,M\times I)\times\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}(P\times I,M\times I)\right),

\displaystyle\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N^{\prime}}\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P\times I\times J,M\times I\times J)\simeq_{[\frac{1}{2}]}\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N^{\prime}}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P\times I\times J,M\times I\times J)\\
\times\\
\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}(P\times I\times J,M\times I\times J)\end{pmatrix}.

Proof.

Suppose given a map of fibration sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\simeq*}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

\scriptstyle\sim

such that
f
is nullhomotopic and
b
is an equivalence. If
\delta:\Omega B\to F
(and similarly for
\delta^{\prime}
) denotes the connecting map, then it follows that
\delta^{\prime}\circ\Omega b\simeq f\circ\delta\simeq*
, and thus
\Omega b
lifts, up to homotopy, to a map
\tilde{\sigma}:\Omega B\to\Omega E^{\prime}
. Then for
(\Omega b)^{-1}
any homotopy inverse to
\Omega b
, the map
\sigma:=\tilde{\sigma}\circ(\Omega b)^{-1}:\Omega B^{\prime}\to\Omega E^{\prime}
is a homotopy section of the fibration
\Omega F^{\prime}\to\Omega E^{\prime}\to\Omega B^{\prime}
and so provides a splitting
\Omega E^{\prime}\simeq\Omega B^{\prime}\times\Omega F^{\prime}
.

For now, let us focus solely on the first equivalence of the statement. By the previous argument, it suffices to show that the leftmost vertical graphing map in the diagram of fibration sequences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}(P\times I,M\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P\times I,M\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P\times I,M\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}

\scriptstyle\sim

is nullhomotopic after localising away from
2
and taking
(\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d+1,p+1)-1)
-th Postnikov sections.

By Proposition
3.3
, we may assume that
\dim P=\dim M=d
. Then, similar to Notation
2.6
, let
\Omega F(-)
and
FI(-)
denote the orthogonal functors given by

\displaystyle\Omega F(V):

\displaystyle=\Omega\operatorname{hofib}(B\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(\overline{M-P}\times V)\to B\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times V)),

\displaystyle FI(V):

\displaystyle=\operatorname{hofib}(B\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(\overline{M-P}\times I\times V)\to B\mathrm{Diff}^{b}_{\partial}(M\times I\times V)).

By taking fibres of the (delooped) graphing maps introduced at the beginning of the section, we obtain a natural transformation
\Gamma:\Omega F(-)\to FI(-)
of orthogonal functors, giving rise to a map of
O(1)
-spectra
\Gamma:\Theta(\Omega F)^{(1)}\longrightarrow\Theta FI^{(1)}
and a commutative diagram

(4.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}(P\times I,M\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(\Theta FI^{(1)}_{hO(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\Theta FI^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial_{0}}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(\Theta(\Omega F)^{(1)}_{hO(1)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\Theta(\Omega F)^{(1)},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{FI}_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Trf}_{O(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\Omega F}_{\infty}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Trf}_{O(1)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}

where
\mathrm{Trf}_{O(1)}
is the
O(1)
-
transfer map
. It is well known that this map is injective in the homotopy category of infinite loop spaces at odd primes. Therefore, since the map
\Phi^{FI}_{\infty}
is
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d+1,p+1)
-connected by Section
3.4
(and thus becomes an equivalence after taking
(\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d+1,p+1)-1)
-th Postnikov sections), it will suffice to show that the rightmost vertical map in (

) is nullhomotopic. By (
3.7
), we have that
\Omega^{\infty}\Theta(\Omega F)^{(1)}\simeq\Omega\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P,M)
and
\Omega^{\infty}\Theta FI^{(1)}\simeq\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P\times I,M\times I)
and, under these equivalences, the right vertical map in (

) then becomes the graphing map

(4.3)

\Gamma:\Omega\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P,M)\longrightarrow\mathcal{CE}\mathrm{mb}(P\times I,M\times I).

This is because both the concordance stabilisation map and the Alexander trick-like map of Proposition
3.11
that give rise to the previous equivalences commute on the nose with the graphing maps, i.e., the following diagrams commute:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times D^{n+1},M\times D^{n+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times I\times D^{n+1},M\times I\times D^{n+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times D^{n},M\times D^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times I\times D^{n},M\times I\times D^{n}),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{n}\Theta(\Omega F)^{(1)}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{n}\Theta FI^{(1)}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times D^{n},M\times D^{n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times I\times D^{n},M\times I\times D^{n}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

So in order to show that (
4.3
) is nullhomotopic, it suffices to argue that it is so unstably, i.e. that the graphing maps

\Gamma:\Omega C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times D^{n},M\times D^{n})\longrightarrow C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times I\times D^{n},M\times I\times D^{n})

are nullhomotopic for all
n\geq 0
. Replacing
M\times D^{n}
by
M
, we may assume
n=0
. This claim is a consequence of the following trick, due to Oscar Randal-Williams: there is a ”
U
-shaped graphing map”

U\Gamma:\Omega C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M)\longrightarrow C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times I,M\times I),

which is homotopic to the standard
\Gamma
by pulling down the
U
-shape to the base of the concordance. This homotopy is illustrated in Figure
1
, where we replace
C\mathrm{Emb}(-,-)
by standard concordances
C(-)
because it is easier to depict, but the idea is the same. Observe that, throughout the homotopy, there are no issues about smoothness in the upper corners because the concordances are equal to the identity near these. Here we are explicitly using the collared condition imposed by the functor
\Omega^{\mathsf{col,sm}}(-)
(see Remark
4.1
).

Figure 1
.
Images of
\gamma\in\Omega C(M)
under the graphing maps
\Gamma
and
U\Gamma
, and the homotopy between them. The concordances are equal to the identity on grey shaded regions.

But clearly
U\Gamma
factors through the path space
\mathrm{Map}(I,C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M))
, and hence there is a homotopy commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times I,M\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times I,M\times I)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Map}(I,C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M),}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega C\mathrm{Emb}(P,M)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U\Gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{ev}_{0}=*}

which exhibits the leftmost vertical map as nullhomotopic, as desired. This establishes the first equivalence of the statement.

Observe now that for
E(-,-)
any of the mapping spaces involved in the proof up until now, the space
E(P\times J,M\times J)
is a group-like topological monoid with respect to stacking in the
J
-direction. Then replacing
(M,P)
by
(M\times J,P\times J)
, one checks that each of the steps in the previous argument can be delooped with respect to this
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-structure. This results in getting rid of the loopings in the first equivalence of the statement, thus yielding the second one. This finishes the proof of the theorem.
∎

The following result will be used to establish the splitting part of Theorem
B
.

Corollary 4.3
.

For
2\leq p\leq d-3
and
N:=\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)-1
, there is an equivalence away from
2

\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N}\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})\simeq_{[\frac{1}{2}]}\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N}\left(\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})\times\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})\right).

For
p=1
, such equivalence just exists after looping, i.e.,

\Omega\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N}\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{1},D^{d})\simeq_{[\frac{1}{2}]}\Omega\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N}\left(\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(D^{1},D^{d})\times\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial}(D^{1},D^{d})\right).

Proof.

When
p\geq 2
, set
(M,P)=(D^{d-2},D^{p-2})
in the second equivalence of Theorem
4.2
. For
p=1
, set
(M,P)=(D^{d-1},D^{0})
in the first equivalence of the same theorem.
∎

5.
Involutions in algebraic
K
-theory

The aim of this section is to explore the involutions of the
C_{2}
-spectra involved in the statements of Theorems
1.1
and
A
, and to express them in terms of simpler and more computable involutions coming from algebraic
K
-theory—the main result in this direction is Theorem
5.11
, which is further simplified by Proposition
5.19
in the case of a suspension. This will then be used in Section
6
to study the case
(M,P)=(D^{d},D^{p})
. As we will shortly see in Section
5.1
, it will be significantly helpful to invert the prime
2
in the analysis of these involutions. Let us now introduce the notation that will be relevant in this section.

Notation 5.1
.

(
i
)  For
M
a compact (smooth) manifold and
\iota:P\subset M
a compact submanifold, recall from Notation
2.6
the definitions of the
C_{2}
-spectra
\mathbf{H}(M)
, the
h
-cobordism spectrum
of
M
, and
\mathbf{CE}(P,M)
, the
concordance embedding spectrum
of
\iota:P\xhookrightarrow{}M
. We refer to their involutions by
\tau_{WW}
, for Weiss–Williams.

(
ii
)  Given a space
X
and a spherical fibration
\xi
over
X
equipped with a section, Vogell defined in
[
Vog85
, p. 300]
an involution
\tau_{\xi}
on
5
5

5

Vogell defined
\tau_{\xi}
on the
A
-theory space
A(X)
, but this involution can be upgraded to
\mathbf{A}(X)
by specifying it on the Waldhausen category of retractive spaces over
X
”with
\xi
-duality” and appealing to the definition of algebraic
K
-theory via the
S_{\bullet}
-construction.

\mathbf{A}(X)
by means of Spanier–Whitehead duality with respect to the Thom spectrum of
\xi
; we will write
\mathbf{A}(X;\xi)
for the corresponding
C_{2}
-spectrum. Under the well-known splitting

(5.1)

\mathbf{A}(X)\simeq\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}X\vee\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(X),

the involution
\tau_{\xi}
descends to one on
\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(X)
; call it
\tau_{\xi}
too. We will write
\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(X;\xi)
for the corresponding
C_{2}
-spectrum. When
\xi=\epsilon:=X\times S^{0}
is the trivial
0
-dimensional sphere bundle, we will refer to
\tau_{\epsilon}
as the
canonical involution
of
K
-theory, and sometimes write
\mathbf{A}(X)
and
\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(X)
for
\mathbf{A}(X;\epsilon)
and
\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(X;\epsilon)
. We will recall a construction of
\tau_{\epsilon}
in terms of Spanier–Whitehead duality in Section
5.4
.

5.1.
Homotopy involutions

A
homotopy involution

\tau
on a space or infinite loop space or spectrum
X
is a self-map
\tau:X\to X
whose square
\tau^{2}
is homotopic to the identity
\mathrm{Id}_{X}
. In this section we explain why, in the stable setting and once the prime
2
is inverted, an involution carries the same amount of information as its underlying homotopy involution. This will be very useful when comparing the
C_{2}
-spectra
\mathbf{H}(M)
and
\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M;\epsilon)
(see Proposition
5.15
). Let us fix some notation first.

Notation 5.2
.

(
i
) Let
\mathsf{C}
denote any of
\mathsf{Top}_{*}
,
\Omega^{\infty}
-
\mathsf{Top}
or
\mathsf{Sp}
, and let
X,X^{\prime}\in\mathsf{C}
be equipped with homotopy involutions
\tau
and
\tau^{\prime}
, respectively. A map
f:X\to X^{\prime}
will be said to be
homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant
, or
C_{2}
-equivariant up to homotopy
, if
f\tau\simeq\tau^{\prime}f
. If
X
and
X^{\prime}
can be connected by a zig-zag of homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant weak equivalences, we will say that
X
and
X^{\prime}
are
homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariantly equivalent
and write

X\approx X^{\prime}.

A
C_{2}
-equivariant equivalence
will always mean a zig-zag of weak equivalences which are
C_{2}
-equivariant.

(
ii
)  An
H
-group

(X,\mu)
is a group-like
\mathbb{A}_{3}
-space (i.e. a homotopy associative
H
-space such that
\pi_{0}(X)
is a group with respect to
\mu
). Given
H
-spaces
(X,\mu)
and
(X^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
, a based map
f:X\to X^{\prime}
will be said to be
monoidal up to homotopy
, or simply an
H
-map
, if the following diagram is homotopy commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}\times X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\times f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

An
equivalence of
H
-groups
will mean a zig-zag of
H
-maps that are additionally weak equivalences. In practice, all
H
-groups we will consider are actually
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-groups (i.e. group-like
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-spaces), and all
H
-maps can be upgraded to
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-maps even though we will not need this.

(
iii
)  The functor
(-)_{hC_{2}}
will always stand for pointed homotopy
C_{2}
-orbits.

In the cases of interest to us and once the prime
2
is inverted, taking homotopy
C_{2}
-orbits with respect to a homotopy involution turns out to make sense.

.

X
and
X^{\prime}

H

\tau
