-coordinates, and the others by their own infinite loop structures.

Claim 1
.

All of the maps in (
5.13
) are
H
-maps if
k\geq 1
.

Proof.

The
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra structure
+_{I}
on
\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{H}(M\times I^{k})
is equivalent to any of the other ones coming from its infinite loop structure. As (
5.11
) and the right vertical equivalence are infinite loop maps, they are in particular
H
-maps. As for (
5.2
), it is an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-map since
(\ref{alexhcob}):H(M\times I^{k})\to\widehat{\mathbf{H}}(M\times I^{k})_{0}
is (by construction).

Non equivariantly, the map
(\dagger)
is the composition
(\dagger):H(M\times I^{k})\xhookrightarrow{}\mathcal{H}(M\times I^{k})\simeq\Omega^{\infty+1}\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M\times I^{k})
, where the last equivalence is the stable parametrised
h
-cobordism theorem of Waldhausen–Jahren–Rognes
[
WJR13b
, Thm. 0.1]
. As communicated to us in private by Bjørn Jahren and John Rognes, such equivalence is only stated to hold in the category of spaces (and not of infinite loop spaces, though it should definitely also hold there). We now explain why this equivalence is one of
H
-groups (which is the general consensus, but we couldn’t find it written down anywhere): again assume
k=1
. One reduces to the
PL
-case as in
[
WJR13b
, pp. 15 & 16]
. Then if
X
is a simplicial set such that
|X|\simeq M
, the equivalence in the
PL
-setting is induced by a zig-zag of equivalences of simplicial sets (cf. the left vertical column of
[
WJR13b
, Eq. (0.4)]
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}(M\times I)_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdot}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s\mathscr{C}^{h}(X\times I),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where
s\mathscr{C}^{h}(X\times I)
is the category (seen as a simplicial set by taking its nerve) of acyclic cofibrations
X\times I\xhookrightarrow{}Y
together with simple maps over
X\times I
. One can verify that it makes sense to stack in the
I
-direction in each of the simplicial sets involved in the zig-zag, and that the maps between them respect this monoidal structure. Let
\mu_{0}:s\mathscr{C}^{h}(X\times I)\times s\mathscr{C}^{h}(X\times I)\to s\mathscr{C}^{h}(X\times I)
stand for this monoidal structure, given explicitly by

\mu_{0}(Y,Z):=Y{}_{X\times{1}}\cup_{X\times 0}Z
. There is another monoidal structure
\mu_{1}
induced by the pushout along
X\times I
, i.e.
\mu_{1}(Y,Z):=Y\cup_{X\times I}Z
. Sliding gives a homotopy between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
: intuitively, the maps

\mu_{t}:s\mathscr{C}^{h}(X\times I)\times s\mathscr{C}^{h}(X\times I)\longrightarrow s\mathscr{C}^{h}(X\times I),\quad(Y,Z)\longmapsto Y{}_{X\times[1-t,1]}\cup_{X\times[0,t]}Z,\quad t\in[0,1],

constitute the homotopy. More precisely, consider the simplicial category
s\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{h}_{\bullet}(X)

[
WJR13b
, Defn. 3.1.1]
whose objects in simplicial degree
q
consist of commutative diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times\Delta^{q}_{\bullet}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{q}_{\bullet},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
i
is an acyclic cofibration and
\pi
is a Serre fibration. The inclusion
s\mathscr{C}^{h}(X)\xhookrightarrow{}s\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{h}_{\bullet}(X)
as the
0
-simplices is a homotopy equivalence by
[
WJR13b
, Cor. 3.5.2]
, where a simplicial category is seen as a bisimplicial set by taking its nerve, and a bisimplicial set as an ordinary simplicial set by taking its totalisation. The monoidal structures
\mu_{t}
make perfectly good sense in
s\widetilde{\mathscr{C}}^{h}_{\bullet}(X\times I)
, and one can indeed define a simplicial homotopy between
\mu_{0}
and
\mu_{1}
in this setting resembling the idea above.

But by Proposition 3.1.1, and Theorems 3.1.7 and 3.3.1 of
[
Wal85
]
(see also
[
WJR13b
, p. 5]
), for any simplicial set
T
, there is a zig-zag of equivalences connecting
|s\mathscr{C}^{h}(T)|
and
\Omega\mathrm{Wh}^{PL}(T):=\Omega^{\infty+1}(\mathbf{Wh}^{PL}(T))
which is monoidal up to homotopy with respect to
\mu_{1}
in the domain and the loop structure on the looped Whitehead space. It hence follows that (
\dagger
) is indeed a zig-zag of
H
-maps.
∎

Claim 2
.

For each
k\geq 1
, the diagram

(5.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(S^{2k\cdot(\sigma-1)}\wedge\mathbf{H}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(M\times I^{2k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(S^{(d+2k)\cdot(\sigma-1)-1}\wedge\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(S^{(2k+2)\cdot(\sigma-1)}\wedge\mathbf{H}(M))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(M\times I^{2k+2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(S^{(d+2k+2)\cdot(\sigma-1)-1}\wedge\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M))}

\approx

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma^{2}}

\approx

is homotopy commutative, where the rows are the zig-zags (
5.13
) and the vertical external maps are induced by the homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant equivalence
\mathbb{S}^{0}\approx\mathbb{S}^{2\cdot(\sigma-1)}
.

Proof.

The right hand square is clearly commutative, for both right horizontal maps factor through
\mathcal{H}(M)
. For the commutativity of the left one, by Lemma
5.13
, it suffices to argue that the (non-equivariant) square

(5.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(M\times I^{2k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{H}(M\times I^{2k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H(M\times I^{2k+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{H}(M\times I^{2k+1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

homotopy commutes. Recall that the map
e_{1}
, non-equivariantly, is induced by the zig-zags

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}(M\times I^{2k})_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\mathbf{H}(M\times I^{2k})_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}(M\times I^{2k+1})_{n},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where
s^{\vee}
is the (adjoint to the) structure map of
\mathbf{H}(M\times I^{2k})
. Since (
5.15
) is a diagram of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-groups by stacking in the first of the
I^{2k}
-coordinates, it suffices to provide a homotopy for the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{s}(M\times I^{2k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}(M\times I^{2k})_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{s}(M\times I^{2k+1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{H}(M\times I^{2k+1})_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\mathbf{H}(M\times I^{2k})_{1}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ref{alexhcob})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{\vee}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ref{alexhcob})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{alex}}

As in Remark
3.9
, such homotopy is obtained by delooping (with respect to stacking in the second of the
I^{2k}
-coordinates) the diagram (
3.5
) of Proposition
3.8
.
∎

Clearly
\Sigma^{2}
is an
H
-map and also homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant (with respect to
\iota_{H}
) by Lemma
5.14
(
b
). Therefore by Claim
1
, all of the maps involved in (
5.14
) are
H
-maps and homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant. Taking the homotopy colimit as
k\to\infty
, we obtain a homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant zig-zag

(5.16)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{H}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{k}{\hocolim}\ H(M\times I^{2k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(S^{d\cdot(\sigma-1)-1}\wedge\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\approx}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\approx}

of
H
-maps. The connectivity of, say, the upper horizontal maps in (
5.14
) is
\phi(d+2k)\gtrsim(d+2k)/3
by Igusa’s theorem and, as this lower bound increases linearly with
k
, the horizontal maps in (
5.16
) are indeed equivalences. The equivalence in the statement now follows by the second part of Proposition
5.3
applied to (
5.16
), and because taking homotopy
C_{2}
-orbits commutes up to equivalence with
\Omega^{\infty}(-)
if
2
is inverted (as in Corollary
5.4
). The proof of Theorem
5.11
is now complete.
∎

Corollary 5.15
.

If
M^{d}
is stably paralellisable and
P\subset M^{d}
is a codimension zero submanifold with
p\leq d-3
(in the notation of Theorem
A
), then there is an equivalence away from two

\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbf{CE}(P,M)_{hC_{2}}\big)\simeq_{[\frac{1}{2}]}\Omega^{\infty}\big(\big(S^{d\cdot(\sigma-1)-2}\wedge\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M,M-P;\epsilon)\big)_{hC_{2}}\big),

where
\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M,M-P;\epsilon)
stands for the homotopy cofibre of
\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M-P;\epsilon)\to\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M;\epsilon)
.

Proof.

Note that
\overline{M-P}
is stably parallelisable because
M
is. The zig-zag (
5.16
) is functorial with respect to codimension zero embeddings of stably paralellisable manifolds, and hence taking homotopy fibres in the map from
(\ref{zigzaginvolutions})
with
M
replaced by
\overline{M-P}
to
(\ref{zigzaginvolutions})
itself, we obtain another homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant zig-zag of equivalences

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{CE}(P,M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underset{k}{\hocolim}\ C\mathrm{Emb}(P\times I^{2k},M\times I^{2k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}(S^{d\cdot(\sigma-1)-2}\wedge\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M,M-P)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\approx}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\approx}

The same line of reasoning as before yields the desired result.
∎

5.4.
The canonical involution in algebraic
K
-theory

We now define the canonical involution
\tau_{\epsilon}
on
A(X)
and explore its connection to an involution in the model of
A
-theory via ”spaces of matrices with values in the ring up to homotopy”
Q_{+}\Omega X

[
Wal85
, §2.2]
. All throughout, let
G:=GX
denote the topological monoid of Moore loops on
X
, and write
\mathbb{S}[G]
for the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-ring spectrum
\mathbb{S}\wedge G_{+}
.

We will work over the
\infty
-category
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}
of right
\mathbb{S}[G]
-module spectra; we will also write
{}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\mathsf{Mod}
for the
\infty
-category of left
\mathbb{S}[G]
-modules. Then for
m\geq 1
, if
\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])
denotes the homotopy invertible components of the mapping space
\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G],\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])
, Waldhausen showed in
[
Wal85
, Thm. 2.2.1]
that for
X
connected, there is a natural equivalence

(5.17)

A(X)\simeq{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}\times\underset{m}{\hocolim}\ B\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])^{+}.

In order to define
\tau_{\epsilon}
, we will introduce compatible anti-involutions on
\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])
defined in terms of Spanier–Whitehead duality. As
\mathbb{S}[G]
is not commutative, this duality really arises as an instance of a
duality in the symmetric closed bicategory

\mathsf{Bimod}{S}
of bimodule spectra, in the sense of
[
MS06
, §16.4]
. This duality coincides with the one considered by Vogell in
[
Vog85
, §1]
.

First observe that a right
\mathbb{S}[G]
-module
M
can always be regarded as a left
\mathbb{S}[G]
-module by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]\otimes M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M\otimes\mathbb{S}[G^{\mathrm{op}}]=M\otimes\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M,}
swap
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Id}_{M}\otimes\text{inv}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{act}}

where ”inv” stands for inversion in the monoid
G
–write
M_{\ell}
for this left
\mathbb{S}[G]
-module. Here
\otimes=\otimes{S}
stands for the usual smash product of spectra. Note also that
{}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\mathsf{Mod}(M_{\ell},M_{\ell})\simeq\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(M,M)
as
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebras. If
\nu:\mathbb{S}\to\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}
denotes the unit, consider the map of spectra

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{1}:\mathbb{S}\simeq\mathbb{S}\otimes_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]\otimes_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu\otimes 1}

and the map of
(\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G])
-bimodules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{1}:\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\otimes\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]\otimes\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{inv}\otimes 1}
act

Then
(\eta_{1},I_{1})
make
(\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell})
a dual pair
[
MS06
, Defn. 16.4.1]
. More generally, the map of spectra

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{m}:\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i,j=1}^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]\otimes_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\cong\bigoplus^{m}_{j=1}\mathbb{S}[G]\otimes_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\big(\bigoplus^{m}_{i=1}\mathbb{S}[G]\big)_{\ell}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i,j}\delta_{ij}\eta_{1}}

together with the map of
(\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G])
-bimodules

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{m}:\big(\bigoplus^{m}_{i=1}\mathbb{S}[G]\big)_{\ell}\otimes\bigoplus^{m}_{j=1}\mathbb{S}[G]\cong\bigoplus_{i,j=1}^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\otimes\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G],}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i,j}\delta_{ij}I_{1}}

exhibit
(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])_{\ell}
as a
right
dual to
\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]
. Therefore as in
[
MS06
, Prop. 16.4.9]
,
I_{m}
induces an equivalence of left
\mathbb{S}[G]
-modules
\widetilde{I}_{m}:(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])_{\ell}\simeq D_{r}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]):=\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G])
, where the right hand side stands for the right
\mathbb{S}[G]
-linear mapping spectrum.

With (
5.17
) and Lemma
5.5
in mind, the involution
\tau_{\epsilon}
on
A(X;\epsilon)
is then induced by the map of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebras

(5.18)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}_{G}\mathrm{Aut}(D_{r}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]))^{\mathrm{op}}\overset{\widetilde{I}_{\#}}{\simeq}{}_{G}\mathrm{Aut}((\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])_{\ell})^{\mathrm{op}}\simeq\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])^{\mathrm{op}},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D_{r}}

where
\widetilde{I}_{\#}
stands for conjugation with the equivalence
\widetilde{I}_{m}
. It will be convenient to think of (
5.18
) in the following way: let
GL_{m}(Q_{+}G)
denote the union of path components in
(Q_{+}G)^{m\times m}
in the image of
\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])
under the natural equivalence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u:\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G],\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G],\prod^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])\simeq\mathsf{Sp}(\mathbb{S},\mathbb{S}[G])^{m\times m}\simeq(Q_{+}G)^{m\times m},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where
\mathsf{Sp}\simeq\mathsf{Mod}{S}
stands for the
\infty
-category of spectra. So
u:\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])\simeq GL_{m}(Q_{+}G)
and, just as in standard linear algebra, under this equivalence the anti-involution (
5.18
) corresponds to the rule that sends a matrix
A
to its conjugate transpose
A^{\dagger}
(conjugate with respect to inversion of
G
in
\mathbb{S}[G]
). More precisely:

Proposition 5.16
.

Write
\mathrm{End}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]):=\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G],\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])
with the action of the cyclic group
C_{m}
by conjugation with the permutation automorphisms of
\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]
. Let
C_{m}
act similarly on
(Q_{+}G)^{m\times m}
by conjugation. Then the following square is commutative in the homotopy category of
C_{m}
-spaces:

(5.19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{End}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{End}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q_{+}G)^{m\times m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Q_{+}G)^{m\times m}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ref{tauepsilonantiinv})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dagger}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Remark 5.17
.

Passing to the homotopy invertible components in (
5.19
), we obtain the following commutative square in the homotopy category of
C_{m}
-spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GL_{m}(Q_{+}G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GL_{m}(Q_{+}G).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ref{tauepsilonantiinv})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\dagger}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

We have suppressed the
(-)^{\mathrm{op}}
in the codomain of the map (
5.18
) in the previous squares to emphasise that such squares take place in the homotopy category of (
C_{m}
-)spaces, and not that of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-spaces.

Proof of Proposition
5.16
.

First note that all the maps involved in (
5.19
) are indeed
C_{m}
-maps: the only one that is not obviously so is (
5.18
), but this follows from the observation that
I_{m}
is
C_{m}
-equivariant for the diagonal action on the domain and the trivial action on the target. Note also that the
C_{m}
-action on
(Q_{+}G)^{m\times m}
restricts to a cofree
C_{m}
-action on each of the right
C_{m}
-cosets of the diagonal subspace, and hence
(Q_{+}G)^{m\times m}=\prod^{m}\mathrm{coInd}_{e}^{C_{m}}Q_{+}G
as a
C_{m}
-space. Thus, in order to show that (
5.19
) commutes in the homotopy category of
C_{m}
-spaces, it suffices to prove that it commutes in the homotopy category of spaces after postcomposing it with the map

(Q_{+}G)^{m\times m}=\prod^{m}\mathrm{coInd}_{e}^{C_{m}}Q_{+}G\longrightarrow\prod^{m}Q_{+}G

that records the first column of a matrix.

Now given an endomorphism
h
of
\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]
, the
(m\times m)
-matrix
u(h)=(h_{ij})\in GL_{m}(Q_{+}G)
has components

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{ij}:\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{inc}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{i}}

Slightly abusing the notation, we will write
\tau_{\epsilon}
to mean (
5.18
). Then we must only check that
\tau_{\epsilon}(h)_{ij}
is homotopic to
\overline{h}_{ji}
, coherently in
h
(and for
j=1
, though it is still true for all
j
of course). Observe now that
(-)_{\ell}:\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\to{}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\mathsf{Mod}
is a functor over
\mathsf{Sp}
, and hence the last equivalence in (
5.18
) happens over the automorphism space of
\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]
as a regular spectrum. Consequently,
\tau_{\epsilon}(h)_{ij}
is by definition the top horizontal composition in the diagram of spectra

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(*_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{inv}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{inc}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{I}_{m}}

\scriptstyle\sim

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(*_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{\epsilon}(h)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(*_{3})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{I}_{m}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{i}}

\simeq

\simeq

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{inv}}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{inc}_{i}^{*}}

On the other hand, one recognises the composition that goes through the bottom row to be
\overline{h}_{ji}
. Note that the left triangle is commutative, and that the square
(*_{2})
is too by definition of
\tau_{\epsilon}(h)
. Moreover, (
*_{1}
) and
(*_{3})
do not depend on
h
; we must then argue that (
*_{1}
) and
(*_{3})
are commutative up to homotopy.

For the commutativity of
(*_{1})
, first observe that the left vertical composition of
(*_{1})
coincides up to homotopy with the equivalence of left
\mathbb{S}[G]
-modules
\widetilde{I}_{1}:\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\simeq\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G])
. This is because, under the usual tensor-hom adjunction, both maps represent the same element in

\pi_{0}\left({}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\mathsf{Mod}\big(\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell},\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G])\big)\right)\cong\pi_{0}\big({}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\big(\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\otimes\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G]\big)\big)

by definition of
I_{1}
. But now (
*_{1}
), with
\widetilde{I}_{1}
in place of the left vertical composition, commutes up to homotopy as both compositions represent the same element in

\pi_{0}\left({}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\mathsf{Mod}\big(\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell},\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G])\big)\right)\cong\pi_{0}\big({}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\mathsf{Mod}_{\mathbb{S}[G]}\big(\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\otimes(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]),\mathbb{S}[G]\big)\big)

simply because the following diagram commutes by definition of
I_{1}
and
I_{m}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\otimes\bigoplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\otimes\bigoplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\otimes\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\mathrm{pr}_{j}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{inc}_{j}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{1}}

Finally the commutativity of (
*_{3}
) follows by a similar reasoning using that

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\otimes\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\otimes\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]_{\ell}\otimes\bigoplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{S}[G]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1\otimes\mathrm{inc}_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{pr}_{i}\otimes 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I_{m}}

is also commutative by definition.
∎

Warning 5.18
.

The canonical involution
\tau_{\epsilon}
induces an involution on the Whitehead group

\mathrm{Wh}(X):=\pi_{1}^{s}(\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(X))\cong GL({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}[\pi_{1}(X)])^{\mathrm{ab}}/(\pm\pi_{1}(X)).

In the foundational paper
[
Mil66
]
, Milnor also defined an involution
\mathrm{Wh}(X)\ni\kappa\mapsto\overline{\kappa}
induced by sending a matrix in
GL({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}[\pi_{1}(X)])^{\mathrm{ab}}
to its conjugate transpose (conjugate with respect to inversion in
\pi_{1}(X)
). This is an actual homomorphism because of the abelianisation present in the general linear group of
{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}[\pi_{1}(X)]
. It is worth being aware that these two involutions on
\mathrm{Wh}(X)
are only the same after introducing a minus sign, i.e.

(5.20)

\tau_{\epsilon}(\kappa)=-\overline{\kappa}.

This does
not
contradict the commutativity of (
5.19
). On the contrary, this extra minus sign is the result of having to deloop the anti-involution (
5.18
) in the sense of Lemma
5.5
in order to obtain
\tau_{\epsilon}
.

5.5.
A
-theory of a suspension

In this section we focus our attention on the homotopy type of
A(X;\epsilon)
when
X
is the suspension
\Sigma Y
of a connected based space
Y
. By a theorem of Carlsson--Cohen--Goodwillie--Hsiang
8
8

8

As pointed out in
[
BCC+96
, p. 543]
, the proof in
[
CCGH87
]
has a serious flaw around p. 71. This issue was fixed in
[
BCC+96
, Cor. 4.15]
, and in particular the map
\theta
of (
5.21
) constructed in
[
CCGH87
, §1]
is still an equivalence. We are indebted to Tom Goodwillie for his help in clearing out this matter and for carefully explaining to us another more general principle for which (
5.21
) holds—namely, it is the observation that if
F
is a functor (from based spaces to based spaces, say) whose
m
-th derivative spectrum is of the form
X\mapsto X^{\wedge m}_{hC_{m}}
for every
m\geq 1
, then its Taylor tower must split globally. This is indeed the case for the functor
F(-):=\Omega\circ A\circ\Sigma(-)
.

[
CCGH87
, Thm. 3]
, in such cases there is an equivalence of infinite loop spaces

(5.21)

\theta:\prod_{m\geq 1}Q(Y^{\wedge m}_{hC_{m}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Omega\widetilde{A}(\Sigma Y),

where
\widetilde{A}(-):=\operatorname{hofib}(A(-)\to A(*))
and
C_{m}
acts on
Y^{\wedge m}
by cyclic permutation of the factors. In this section, we argue that (
5.21
) can be upgraded to be
C_{2}
-equivariant up to homotopy.

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
Y
be a connected, based
C_{2}
-space. There is an equivalence of spectra

\boldsymbol{\theta}:\bigvee_{m\geq 1}\Sigma^{\infty+\sigma}\big((ED_{m})_{+}\wedge_{C_{m}}Y^{\wedge m}\big)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon)

that is
C_{2}
-equivariant up to homotopy, and whose underlying (non-equivariant) equivalence induces (
5.21
). Here
\Sigma^{\sigma}Y:=S^{\sigma}\wedge Y
and
D_{m}\subset\Sigma_{m}
acts on
Y^{\wedge m}
by

g\cdot(y_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge y_{m})\longmapsto g\cdot y_{g(1)}\wedge\dots\wedge g\cdot y_{g(m)},\quad g\in D_{m},\quad y_{i}\in Y,

where
Y
is now seen as a based
D_{m}
-space (on which
C_{m}
acts trivially). Finally
C_{2}=D_{m}/C_{m}
acts on
(ED_{m})_{+}\wedge_{C_{m}}Y^{\wedge m}
by its residual diagonal action.

Remark 5.20
.

The
C_{2}
-space
\Sigma^{\sigma}Y
induces an involution on
\mathbf{A}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y)
which commutes with the canonical involution
\tau_{\epsilon}
described in the previous section, by naturality of its construction. Therefore its composite gives the involution on
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon)
appearing in the statement of Proposition
5.19
. Alternatively, we can allow
X
in the previous section to mean a
C_{2}
-space (e.g.
\Sigma^{\sigma}Y
), and agree that
\mathrm{inv}:G=GX\to G^{\mathrm{op}}
there stands for inversion in the monoid
G
followed by the
C_{2}
-action on
X
.

In practice, we will apply Proposition
5.19
to the case when
Y=S^{\sigma}\wedge Z
for some trivial
C_{2}
-space
Z
, as then
\Sigma^{\sigma}Y\simeq S^{2\sigma}\wedge Z\approx S^{2}\wedge Z
because of the homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant equivalence
S^{2\sigma}\approx S^{2}
. In such case, as
\mathbf{A}(-;\epsilon)
is a homotopy functor, Proposition
5.19
provides a simple description of the homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant homotopy type of
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(\Sigma^{2}Z;\epsilon)\approx\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(\Sigma^{2\sigma}Z;\epsilon)
. This, together with Proposition
5.3
, can then be used to analyse the homotopy type of
\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(\Sigma^{2}Z;\epsilon)_{hC_{2}}
away from
2
.

We will need the following observation for the proof of Proposition
5.19
.

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
X
be a based, connected
C_{m}
-space. The equivalence

\iota:B((C_{m}\ltimes\Omega X)^{\mathrm{op}})\simeq B(C_{m}\ltimes\Omega X)

of Lemma
5.5
coincides up to equivalence with the delooping of the inversion map

\mathrm{inv}:(C_{m}\ltimes\Omega X)^{\mathrm{op}}\longrightarrow C_{m}\ltimes\Omega X,\quad(s^{i},\gamma)\mapsto(s^{-i},s^{i}\cdot\overline{\gamma}),

where
\overline{\gamma}
stands for the loop
\gamma
with the reversed orientation.

Proof.

Given a topological monoid
M
equipped with a
C_{m}
-action, it is well-known (see e.g.
[
AM04
, §II, Thm. 1.12]
) that the classifying space of the semi-direct product
C_{m}\ltimes M
is equivalent to
EC_{m}\times_{C_{m}}BM
. On the simplicial level, this equivalence is given by

\displaystyle\beta:B_{\bullet}(C_{m}\ltimes M)

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}E_{\bullet}C_{m}\times_{C_{m}}B_{\bullet}M,

\displaystyle\big((s^{i_{1}},m_{1}),\dots,(s^{i_{q}},m_{q})\big)

\displaystyle\longmapsto\big[(e,s^{i_{1}},\dots,s^{i_{q}}),(s^{i_{1}}\cdot m_{1},s^{i_{1}+i_{2}}\cdot m_{2},\dots,s^{i_{1}+\dots+i_{q}}\cdot m_{q})\big].

Now, for simplicity, we may assume that
\Omega(-)
stands for the Moore loop space, so that
C_{m}\ltimes\Omega X
is strictly associative (a
Moore loop
is a pair
(\gamma,t)
where
t\geq 0
and
\gamma:[0,t]\to Y
is a map with
\gamma(0)=\gamma(t)=*
; multiplication of Moore loops is given by concatenation of loops and addition of its lengths). Also recall that there is an identification
\Delta^{q}\cong\{\mathbf{v}=(v_{1},\dots,v_{q}):0\leq v_{1}\leq\dots\leq v_{q}\leq 1\}
; under this identification, the self-isomorphism
\Phi_{q}:\Delta^{q}\cong\Delta^{q}
in the proof of Lemma
5.5
becomes the rule that sends a partition
\mathbf{v}=(0\leq v_{1}\leq\dots\leq v_{q}\leq 1)
to
1-\mathbf{v}:=(0\leq 1-v_{q}\leq\dots\leq 1-v_{1}\leq 1)
. There is a
C_{m}
-equivariant map

\xi:B\Omega X\longrightarrow X,\quad\big[(\gamma_{1},t_{1}),\dots,(\gamma_{q},t_{q}),\mathbf{v}\big]\longmapsto\gamma_{q}\cdot\gamma_{q-1}\cdot\dotso\cdot\gamma_{1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{q}t_{i}v_{i}\right)

that is an equivalence if
X
is connected. This map satisfies the property that

\xi\left(\big[(\overline{\gamma}_{1},t_{1}),\dots,(\overline{\gamma}_{q},t_{q}),\mathbf{v}\big]\right)=\xi\left(\big[(\gamma_{q},t_{q}),\dots,(\gamma_{1},t_{1}),1-\mathbf{v}\big]\right).

With all of this in mind, one verifies that the following diagram commutes up to homotopy

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B((C_{m}\ltimes\Omega X)^{\mathrm{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(C_{m}\ltimes\Omega X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces EC_{m}\times_{C_{m}}B\Omega X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(C_{m}\ltimes\Omega X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces EC_{m}\times_{C_{m}}B\Omega X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces EC_{m}\times_{C_{m}}X.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathrm{inv})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces EC_{m}\times_{C_{m}}\xi}

\scriptstyle\sim

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces EC_{m}\times_{C_{m}}\xi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

∎

Proof of Proposition
5.19
.

In the notation of the previous section, we let
X=\Sigma^{\sigma}Y
now, so that
G=GX=\Omega^{\sigma}\Sigma^{\sigma}Y
as a monoid with anti-involution (i.e. ”inv” now means inversion in the monoid
G
followed by the
C_{2}
-action on
X=\Sigma^{\sigma}Y
). For each
m\geq 1
, let us write
\varrho:GL_{m}(Q_{+}G)\simeq\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])
for
u^{-1}
(meaning
u
as a wrong way equivalence); it should be thought of as given by the rule that sends a matrix
h=(h_{ij})\in\operatorname{Map}(\mathbb{S},\mathbb{S}[G])^{m\times m}\cong\operatorname{Map}_{G}(\mathbb{S}[G],\mathbb{S}[G])^{m\times m}
to

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho(h):\bigoplus_{j=1}^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}\mathbb{S}[G].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{j}\big(\bigoplus_{i}h_{ij}\big)}

The map
\theta
of (
5.21
) is constructed in several steps in
[
CCGH87
, §1]
, each of which we now upgrade to the homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant setting. As these homotopy
C_{2}
-actions will get mixed up with strict
C_{m}
-actions, it will be more convenient and clear, at least throughout the first few steps, to avoid speaking about ”homotopy equivariance” and rather regard a homotopy involution as what it is, i.e. a map whose square happens to be homotopic to the identity.

Step 1
.

For each
m\geq 2
, consider the
D_{m}
-equivariant map of spaces

\widetilde{\theta}_{m,1}:Y^{\times m}\longrightarrow GL_{m}(Q_{+}G),\quad(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})\longmapsto\resizebox{9323399}{}{$\begin{pmatrix}1&y_{1}-1&&&&\\
&1&y_{2}-1&&&\\
&&1&\ddots&&\\
&&&\ddots&&\\
&&&&1&y_{m-1}-1\\
y_{m}-1&&&&&1\end{pmatrix}$}

where, if
D_{m}:=\langle s,r\mid s^{m}=r^{2}=rsrs=e\rangle
, the notation is as follows:

•

A point
y\in Y
is identified in
G
with the path
\eta(y):=(t\mapsto t\wedge y)\in G
, which is itself identified with a point in
\{1\}\times QG\subset QS^{0}\times QG\simeq Q_{+}G
. Then
y-1
is the corresponding point in
\{0\}\times QG\subset Q_{+}G
. Here the
n
-th component of
QS^{0}
has been fixed a basepoint
n\in QS^{0}
.

•

The action of
D_{m}
on
(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})\in Y^{\times m}
is given by

s\cdot(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}):=(y_{m},y_{1},\dots,y_{m-1}),\quad r\cdot(y_{1},\dots,y_{m}):=(y^{*}_{m-1},y^{*}_{m-2},\dots,y_{1}^{*},y_{m}^{*}),

where
y\mapsto y^{*}
denotes the
C_{2}
-action on
Y
.

•

Let
S,R\in GL_{m}({\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}})
be the permutation matrices that send the
i
-th unit vector
e_{i}
to
e_{i+1}
and
e_{m+1-i}
(with subindexes taken modulo
m
), respectively. Then
D_{m}
acts on
A\in GL_{m}(Q_{+}G)
by

s\cdot A:=SAS^{-1},\qquad r\cdot A:=RA^{\dagger}R.

In other words,
r
acts by transposition along the ”
x=y
”-axis together with conjugation on
G
.

We also define
\widetilde{\theta}_{1,1}:Y\to GL_{1}(Q_{+}G)=(Q_{+}G)^{\times}
by sending
y\in Y
to
y\in\{1\}\times QG\subset Q_{+}G
. We note that
\widetilde{\theta}_{m,1}(y_{1},\dots,y_{m})
is homotopy invertible by choosing a path from each of the
y_{i}
’s to the basepoint
*\in Y
. Then for
m\geq 1
, define
\theta_{m,1}
as the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{m,1}:Y^{\times m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GL_{m}(Q_{+}G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\theta}_{m,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

By construction,
\theta_{m,1}
is a
C_{m}
-map as both
\widetilde{\theta}_{m,1}
and
u
are. Recall that
s\in C_{m}\subset D_{m}
acts on
\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])
by conjugation with
S\in\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])
, which we denote by
S_{\#}
.

Step 2
.

Recall that the free
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-algebra on a based, connected space
X
is naturally equivalent to
\Omega\Sigma X
. Therefore, we can extend
\theta_{m,1}
to a
C_{m}
-equivariant
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-map

\theta_{m,2}:\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]).

For any based space
X
, let us write
\sigma:\Omega X\to(\Omega X)^{\mathrm{op}}
for inversion in
\Omega X
(i.e. reversing the loop direction). Given a
C_{m}
-space
X
, we will denote
X^{\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}
for
X
with the opposite
C_{m}
-action (i.e. that in which
s
acts by
s^{-1}
, which is a valid left action as
C_{m}
is abelian). If
X
additionally has an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-structure, we will write
X^{\mathrm{op},\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}
for
X
with both the opposite
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-structure and the opposite
C_{m}
-action. Then the square of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-maps

(5.22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m})^{\mathrm{op},\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])^{\mathrm{op},\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\circ r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{m,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\#}\circ\tau_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{m,2}^{\mathrm{op},\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}

commutes in the homotopy category of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-spaces with a
C_{m}
-action. Here
r:\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m})\to\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m})^{\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}
is induced by the action of
r\in D_{m}
on
Y^{\times m}
together with the flip of the suspension coordinate, and
\tau_{\epsilon}
really stands for (
5.18
). To see this, consider the diagram

(5.23)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y^{\times m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GL_{m}(Q_{+}G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(Y^{\times m})^{\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GL_{m}(Q_{+}G)^{\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m})^{\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])^{\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\circ r\circ\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\theta}_{m,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r=R_{\#}\circ\dagger}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\#}\circ\tau_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\theta}_{m,1}^{\hskip 2.0pt\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta^{\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varrho^{\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{m,2}^{\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}

of
C_{m}
-spaces. By definition,
\theta_{m,2}
is the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-map induced from the top horizontal composition in (
5.23
). Thus, in order to show that (
5.22
) homotopy commutes as
C_{m}
-equivariant
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-maps, it suffices to show that the outer square of (
5.23
) commutes in the homotopy category of
C_{m}
-spaces. But each of its subsquares/triangles commute in this category: indeed the lower subsquare does so by definition of
\theta_{m,2}
(after applying
(-)^{\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}
), the left subtriangle and the upper subsquare too by an easy check, and the right subsquare by Proposition
5.16
and the observation that
R_{\#}\circ u=u^{\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}\circ R_{\#}
.

Step 3
.

The
C_{m}
-equivariant
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-map
\theta_{m,2}
gives rise to an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{m,3}:C_{m}\ltimes\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{m}\ltimes\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{m}\ltimes\theta_{m,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}

where
\mu(s^{i},h):=S^{i}h
. Observe that
\mu
is indeed an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-map as

\mu((s^{i},h)\cdot(s^{j},h^{\prime}))=\mu(s^{i+j},S^{-j}hS^{j}h^{\prime})=S^{i}hS^{j}h^{\prime}=\mu(s^{i},h)\mu(s^{j},h^{\prime}).

Now from the homotopy commutativity of (
5.22
), it immediately follows that the left subsquare in

(5.24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{m}\ltimes\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{m}\ltimes\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{m}\ltimes\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m})^{\mathrm{op},\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{m}\ltimes\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])^{\mathrm{op},\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{m}\ltimes(\sigma\circ r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{m,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{m}\ltimes\theta_{m,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{m}\ltimes(R_{\#}\circ\tau_{\epsilon})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\#}\circ\tau_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{m}\ltimes\theta_{m,2}^{\mathrm{op},\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu^{\mathrm{op}}}

commutes in the homotopy category of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-spaces. Here
\mu^{\mathrm{op}}(s^{i},h):=hS^{i}
, and since
\tau_{\epsilon}(S)=S^{\dagger}=S^{-1}
and
RS=S^{-1}R
, it easily follows that the right subsquare also commutes as
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-maps. So the outer square of (
5.24
) commutes in the homotopy category of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-spaces.

But given an
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-space
X
equipped with a
C_{m}
-action, there is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-spaces

\alpha:C_{m}\ltimes X^{\mathrm{op},\mathrm{op}_{C_{m}}}\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}(C_{m}\ltimes X)^{\mathrm{op}},\quad(s^{i},x)\longmapsto(s^{i},s^{-i}\cdot x).

Under this identification, the lower horizontal composition of (
5.24
) becomes
\theta_{m,3}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and hence

(5.25)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{m}\ltimes\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{m}\ltimes\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m}))^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\circ(C_{m}\ltimes(\sigma\circ r))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{m,3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{\#}\circ\tau_{\epsilon}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{m,3}^{\mathrm{op}}}

is commutative in the homotopy category of
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-spaces.

Step 4
.

We wish to deloop (
5.25
), viewing the vertical maps as anti-involutions of their respective domains, and appealing to Lemma
5.5
to do so. But by Lemma
5.21
, the delooping of the anti-involution
\alpha\circ(C_{m}\ltimes(\sigma\circ r))
is homotopic to the delooping of the involution
\mathrm{inv}\circ\alpha\circ(C_{m}\ltimes(\sigma\circ r))
, where
\mathrm{inv}
stands for inversion in the
\mathbb{E}_{1}
-space
C_{m}\ltimes\Omega\Sigma(Y^{\times m})
. It is given explictly by
\mathrm{inv}(s^{i},\gamma):=(s^{-i},s^{i}\cdot\sigma(\gamma))
, and hence we see that

\mathrm{inv}\circ\alpha\circ(C_{m}\ltimes(\sigma\circ r)):(s^{i},\gamma)\longmapsto(s^{-i},r\cdot\gamma).

We denote this map simply by
\mathrm{inv}\ltimes r
. From now on we treat
r
as the action map on the
D_{m}
-space
\Sigma^{\sigma}(Y^{\times m})
, where
\sigma
is seen as a
D_{m}
-representation on which
C_{m}
acts trivially. Putting this together, the delooped version of (
5.25
) yields a homotopy commutative square of spaces

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(C_{m}\ltimes\Omega\Sigma^{\sigma}(Y^{\times m}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(C_{m}\ltimes\Omega\Sigma^{\sigma}(Y^{\times m}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G]),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\mathrm{inv}\ltimes r)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\theta_{m,3})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{B}(R_{\#}\circ\tau_{\epsilon})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B(\theta_{m,3})}

where the notation
\overline{B}(-)
stands for delooping in the sense of Lemma
5.5
.

To simplify the terms in this last diagram, first observe that as
Y^{\times m}
is connected, we have

B(C_{m}\ltimes\Omega\Sigma^{\sigma}(Y^{\times m}))\simeq ED_{m}\times_{C_{m}}B\Omega\Sigma^{\sigma}(Y^{\times m})\simeq ED_{m}\times_{C_{m}}\Sigma^{\sigma}(Y^{\times m}).

The inversion on
C_{m}
coincides with the residual
C_{2}=D_{m}/C_{m}
-action on
C_{m}
by conjugation, which explains why we chose to write
ED_{m}
instead of
EC_{m}
. As for the right hand side, note that
R_{\#}
is an inner automorphism of
\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])
, and hence it induces a map homotopic to the identity on the classifying space level
[
AM04
, §II, Thm. 1.9]
. But delooping is functorial, so
\overline{B}(R_{\#}\circ\tau_{\epsilon})
and
\overline{B}(\tau_{\epsilon})=:\tau_{\epsilon}
are homotopic involutions on
B\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])
. All together, we obtain a homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant map

\theta_{m,4}:ED_{m}\times_{C_{m}}\Sigma^{\sigma}(Y^{\times m})\xlongrightarrow{B(\theta_{m,3})^{+}}B\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])^{+}\subset{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}\times B\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])^{+}\to A(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon),

where the last map is the passage to the colimit as
m\to\infty
(see (
5.17
)).

Step 5
.

The following diagram commutes up to homotopy:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{m,4}:ED_{m}/C_{m}\simeq BC_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(*;\epsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{m,4}:ED_{m}\times_{C_{m}}\Sigma^{\sigma}(Y^{\times m})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S}[G])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Bj}

where
j:C_{m}\to\mathrm{Aut}_{G}(\oplus^{m}\mathbb{S})
is the inclusion of the permutation automorphisms. As
A(-;\epsilon)=\Omega^{\infty}\mathbf{A}(-;\epsilon)
, we can adjoin the
\Omega^{\infty}(-)
to get a similar homotopy commutative diagram of spectra. Then passing to vertical cofibres and noting that
C_{m}
acts trivially on the suspension coordinate of
\Sigma^{\sigma}(Y^{\times m})
, we get a homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant map of spectra

\theta_{m,5}:\Sigma^{\infty+\sigma}\big((ED_{m})_{+}\wedge_{C_{m}}Y^{\times m}\big)\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon).

Step 6
.

Now by
[
CCGH87
, Lem. 1.4]
(see also
[
CC87
, Lem. 2.4]
), the obvious projection
(ED_{m})_{+}\wedge_{C_{m}}Y^{\times m}\to(ED_{m})_{+}\wedge_{C_{m}}Y^{\wedge m}
has a stable section
\Sigma^{\infty}\big((ED_{m})_{+}\wedge_{C_{m}}Y^{\wedge m}\big)\to\Sigma^{\infty}\big((ED_{m})_{+}\wedge_{C_{m}}Y^{\times m}\big)
that is
D_{m}/C_{m}
-equivariant. This observation gives rise to a homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant map of spectra

\boldsymbol{\theta}_{m,6}:\Sigma^{\infty+\sigma}\big((ED_{m})_{+}\wedge_{C_{m}}Y^{\wedge m}\big)\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon).

Finally set
\boldsymbol{\theta}
to be

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\theta}:\bigvee_{m\geq 1}\Sigma^{\infty+\sigma}\big((ED_{m})_{+}\wedge_{C_{m}}Y^{\wedge m}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee_{m\geq 1}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigvee_{m\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\displaystyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\textstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle\geq$}}1}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{m,6})}

This map is homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant by construction, and non-equivariantly yields (
5.21
) after applying
\Omega^{\infty+\sigma}(-)
. This latter map is an equivalence of infinite loop spaces by
[
CCGH87
, Thm. 1.6]
, and as both the domain and codomain of
\boldsymbol{\theta}
are
1
-connective, it follows that
\boldsymbol{\theta}
is itself an equivalence of spectra. This concludes the proof of Proposition
5.19
.
∎

Corollary 5.22
.

Let
Y
be a connected, based
C_{2}
-space. There is an equivalence of spectra

\boldsymbol{\theta}:\bigvee_{m\geq 2}\Sigma^{\infty+\sigma}\big((ED_{m})_{+}\wedge_{C_{m}}Y^{\wedge m}\big)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\widetilde{\mathbf{Wh}}{\vphantom{\mathrm{Wh}}}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon)

that is
C_{2}
-equivariant up to homotopy. Here
\widetilde{\mathbf{Wh}}{\vphantom{\mathrm{Wh}}}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(-):=\operatorname{hofib}(\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(-)\to\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(*))
.

Proof.

It is clear from the construction that the map

\Sigma^{\infty}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y)\simeq\Sigma^{\infty+\sigma}\big((ED_{1})_{+}\wedge_{C_{1}}Y^{\wedge 1}\big)\xlongrightarrow{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{1,6}}\widetilde{\mathbf{A}}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon)

is the (reduced version of the) usual inclusion of the stable homotopy into
A
-theory. Thus its cofibre is
\widetilde{\mathbf{Wh}}{\vphantom{\mathrm{Wh}}}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon)
, and the claim follows immediately.
∎

Remark 5.23
.

As the reader may have noticed by now, the last two sections are a tiny bit technical, and one may wonder if there could be alternative approaches to deal with them. Such an approach that may come to mind is to use trace methods to analyse
\widetilde{A}(\Sigma^{\sigma}Y;\epsilon)
, since it coincides (non-equivariantly) with the reduced
TC
of
\mathbb{S}[\Omega\Sigma Y]
(as
Y
is connected). In fact, recent developments have been made towards a (genuine)
C_{2}
-equivariant version of topological cyclic homology for ring spectra with anti-involutions, commonly known as
real topological cyclic homology
(cf.
[
Hø16
,
HM16
,
DMP21
]
). This approach has two caveats:

•

A
real cyclotomic trace
map does not yet exist (at the time of writing). The construction of such a map was supposed to appear in
[
HM16
]
, but it never saw the light in the end. This is, nevertheless, current work in progress by Harpaz–Nikolaus–Shah
[
HNS21
, p. 24]
.

•

Even though much is known about the
p
-complete homotopy type of the
TC
of spherical group rings (cf.
[
BCC+96
]
or
[
NS18
, §4.3]
), the analysis of its integral homotopy type does not seem to be present in the literature.

For these two reasons, we preferred to proceed as we have.

6.
The homotopy type of spaces of long knots

This section is devoted to Theorem
B
, which describes the homotopy type of
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
for
p\leq d-3
and
d\geq 5
, localised at odd primes and up to the concordance embedding stable range
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)
. After its proof, which will not take too much effort given the results in the preceeding sections, we will draw some conclusions on the homotopy groups of spaces of long knots. For convenience let us recall the statement of Theorem
B
. Recall that
\psi_{m}
stands for the real
m
-dimensional representation of the dihedral group
D_{m}
and
\sigma
for the sign representation, regarded in this statement as a
D_{m}
-representation by restricting along the determinant
D_{m}\xhookrightarrow{}O(2)\overset{\det}{\to}\{\pm 1\}=C_{2}
.

Theorem

(Theorem
B
)
.

For
p\leq d-3
and
d\geq 5
, consider the virtual
D_{m}
-representations

\rho_{m}:=(d+1)(\sigma-1)+\psi_{m}\otimes(d-p-3+\sigma).

Then the homotopy fibre sequence (
6.1
), upon localising away from
2
and taking
(\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)-1)
-th Postnikov sections, takes the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{m\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\geq$}}2}\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{p}\operatorname{hofib}\big(G(d-p)/O(d-p)\to G/O\big).}

The resulting sequence is split if
p\geq 2
, and splits after being looped once if
p=1
.

Recall from Remark
1.4
(
ii
) what we mean by localising the spaces
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
and
\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
.

6.1.
Proof of Theorem
B

Recall from (
1.4
) that we write
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}^{(\sim)}(P,M):=\operatorname{hofib}_{\iota}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(P,M)\to\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(P,M))
when
\iota
is clear from the context. We saw in Corollary
4.3
that the fibration sequence

(6.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d}),}

upon localising at odd primes and taking
(\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)-1)
-th Postnikov sections, is split for
2\leq p\leq d-3
, and splits for
p=1
after looping once. So we need to describe the exterior terms of (
6.1
) after inverting
2
.

For the block embeddings, the graphing map

(6.2)

\Gamma:\Omega^{p}\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}(*,D^{d-p})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d-p}\times D^{p})\cong\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})

of (
4.1
) is an equivalence by inspection. Then by
[
GKW01
, Thm. 2.2.1]
and the example right after it, when
d-p\geq 3
and
d\geq 5
(see Remark
6.1
below), it follows that

(6.3)

\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})\simeq\Omega^{p}\operatorname{hofib}(O/O(d-p)\to G/G(d-p))\simeq\Omega^{p}\operatorname{hofib}(G(d-p)/O(d-p)\to G/O),

yielding the base of (
6.1
).

Remark 6.1
.

The equivalence (
6.3
) is only valid if
d-p\geq 3
and
d\geq 5
. As pointed out right after
[
GKW01
, Thm. 2.2.1]
, the second condition is not that important. For instance in the case
p=1
and
d=4
, it follows directly from (
6.2
) and (
6.3
) that

\Omega\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(D^{1},D^{4})\simeq\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(D^{2},D^{5})\simeq\Omega^{2}\operatorname{hofib}(G(3)/O(3)\to G/O).

The codimension condition
d-p\geq 3
, however, is essential.

For the fibre of (
6.1
), we know by Theorem
A
that for
N=\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)-1
, there is an equivalence

\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N}\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})\simeq\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N}\ \Omega^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{CE}(D^{p},D^{d})_{hC_{2}}\right).

We now use Corollaries
5.15
and
5.22
to describe the right hand side of the equivalence above.

Proposition 6.2
.

For
\rho_{m}
as in Theorem
B
, there is an equivalence

\Omega^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{CE}(D^{p},D^{d})_{hC_{2}}\right)\simeq_{[\frac{1}{2}]}\prod_{m\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\geq$}}2}\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}}\big).

Proof.

First observe that there is a homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant equivalence
S^{2}\approx S^{2\sigma}
. So by Corollary
5.22
, for each
n\geq 2
there is a homotopy
C_{2}
-equivariant equivalence of spectra

\widetilde{\mathbf{Wh}}{\vphantom{\mathrm{Wh}}}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(S^{n};\epsilon)\approx\widetilde{\mathbf{Wh}}{\vphantom{\mathrm{Wh}}}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(\Sigma^{\sigma}S^{n-2+\sigma};\epsilon)\approx\bigvee_{m\geq 2}\Sigma^{\infty+\sigma}\big(S^{\psi_{m}\otimes(n-2+\sigma)}_{hC_{m}}\big)

Using this for
n=d-p-1\geq 2
, we obtain a chain of equivalences

\displaystyle\Omega^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{CE}(D^{p},D^{d})_{hC_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq_{[\frac{1}{2}]}\Omega^{\infty}\left((S^{d\cdot(\sigma-1)-1}\wedge\widetilde{\mathbf{Wh}}{\vphantom{\mathrm{Wh}}}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(S^{d-p-1}))_{hC_{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq_{[\frac{1}{2}]}\Omega^{\infty}\big(\big(\bigvee_{m\geq 2}\mathbb{S}^{(d+1)\cdot(\sigma-1)}\wedge\mathbb{S}^{\psi_{m}\otimes(n-2+\sigma)}_{hC_{m}}\big)_{hC_{2}}\big).

\displaystyle=\prod_{m\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\geq$}}2}\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}}\big).

The first equivalence follows from Corollary
5.15
, together with the observation that
\widetilde{\mathbf{Wh}}{\vphantom{\mathrm{Wh}}}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(S^{d-p-1})\simeq\Sigma^{-1}\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(D^{p},S^{d-p-1};\epsilon)
as both
\tau_{\epsilon}
and
\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(-)
are homotopy invariants of
(-)
. The second equivalence is a consequence of the previous argument and Proposition
5.3
. This establishes the desired equivalence.
∎

All together, this concludes the proof of Theorem
B
. ∎

Remark 6.3

(Topological version of Theorem
B
)
.

The space
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}^{\mathrm{Top}}(D^{p},D^{d})
of topological long knots is contractible (for all
p\leq d
) by the Alexander trick. We could still be interested in the homotopy type of the space
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{Top}}(D^{p}\times D^{d-p},D^{d})
of thickened topological long knots with
p\leq d-3
, and one can get a description of it localised away from
2
and up to the concordance embedding stable range, similar to the one in Theorem
B
—let us explain how. As before, we have a homotopy fibre sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{Top},(\sim)}(D^{p}\times D^{d-p},D^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{Top}}(D^{p}\times D^{d-p},D^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{Top}}(D^{p}\times D^{d-p},D^{d})}

which, upon localising at odd primes and taking
(\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)-1)
-th Postnikov sections, is split for
2\leq p\leq d-3
, and splits for
p=1
after looping once. So we should describe the side terms.

For the block embeddings, consider the space

B\widetilde{\mathrm{Top}}(q):=\operatorname{holim}\left(\hbox to102.8pt{\vbox to42.42pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 51.40169pt\lower-21.2083pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{}{
{}{}{}}
{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)

which is responsible for the classification of topological block normal bundles if
q\geq 3
(cf.
[
RS68b
, §2]
,
[
RS68a
]
and
[
Wal99
, §11]
). As
\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}^{\mathrm{Top}}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
is contractible by the Alexander trick, it then follows that

\widetilde{\operatorname{Emb}}^{\mathrm{Top}}_{\partial_{0}}(D^{p}\times D^{d-p},D^{d})\simeq\operatorname{Map}_{\partial}(D^{p},\widetilde{\mathrm{Top}}(d-p))=\Omega^{p}\widetilde{\mathrm{Top}}(d-p).

As for the pseudoisotopy embeddings, the topological version of Theorem
A
(see Remark
1.3
) tells us that for
N=\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}(d,p)}-1
, there is an equivalence

(6.4)

\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N}\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial_{0}}^{\mathrm{Top},(\sim)}(D^{p}\times D^{d-p},D^{d}))\simeq\tau_{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\leq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\leq$}}N}\ \Omega^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{CE}^{\mathrm{Top}}(D^{p}\times D^{d-p},D^{d})_{hC_{2}}\right),

where
\mathbf{CE}^{\mathrm{Top}}(P,M)
stands for the first orthogonal derivative of the topological analogue of
F(-)
(as in Corollary
5.15
, this is a
C_{2}
-spectrum whose infinite loop space is equivalent to that of
\Sigma^{-2}\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Top}}(M,M-P;\epsilon)
). Noting that there is fibre sequence of
C_{2}
-spectra
\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(M;\epsilon)\to\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Top}}(M;\epsilon)\to\Sigma\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(*;\epsilon)\wedge M_{+}
, one verifies that the infinite loop space in the right hand side of (
6.4
) fits in a fibre sequence away from
2

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{m\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\geq$}}2}\Omega^{\infty}\big(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{CE}^{\mathrm{Top}}(D^{p}\times D^{d-p},D^{d})_{hC_{2}}\right)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\infty}\big((S^{d\cdot\sigma-p-2}\wedge\mathbf{Wh}^{\mathrm{Diff}}(*;\epsilon))_{hC_{2}}\big).}

In particular, it is easy to check (e.g. rationally) that the left hand side of (
6.4
) is not contractible (at least if
d
is sufficiently large). This was claimed in Remark
3.4
.

6.2.
On the homotopy groups of spaces of long knots

We can get plenty of information about the homotopy groups of
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
from Theorem
B
. First observe that by Morlet’s lemma of disjunction
[
BLR06
, Thm. 3.1]
(and Proposition
3.3
to reduce to the codimension zero case), the pseudoisotopy embedding space
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}^{(\sim)}(D^{p},D^{d})
is at least
(2(d-p-2)-1)
-connected. So by (
6.3
), it follows that

(6.5)

\pi_{*}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d}))\cong\pi_{*+p}(\operatorname{hofib}(G(d-p)/O(d-p)\to G/O)),\quad*<2(d-p-2).

Remark 6.4
.

This should be compared to work of Budney
[
Bud08
, Prop. 3.9]
. The main result there is the computation of the first non-trivial homotopy group of
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
for
d-p\geq 3
, which lies in degree
2d-3p-3
, together with a geometric interpretation of the generators. From our point of view, he shows that
\operatorname{hofib}(G(d-p)/O(d-p)\to G/O)
is exactly
(2d-2p-4)
-connected, which follows by work of Haefliger (see Section 3, Equation 4.11 and Corollary 6.6 of
[
Hae66
]
), and computes
\pi_{2d-2p-3}(\operatorname{hofib}(G(d-p)/O(d-p)\to G/O))
. Moreover, it is stated in
[
Bud08
, Prop. 3.9(1)]
that the graphing map

\Gamma:\pi_{*}(\Omega\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p-1},D^{d-1}))\longrightarrow\pi_{*}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d}))

is surjective for
*\leq 2d-2p-5
. From (
6.3
) and the fact that
\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
is
(2d-2p-5)
-connected, we see that it is in fact an isomorphism.

Recall that
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)\geq 2d-p-5
by work of Goodwillie–Krannich–Kupers
[
GKK23
]
, and so the space
\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
has interesting homotopy in degrees from
2d-2p-4
up to that range that we can understand by Theorem
B
. For any odd prime
\ell
there are isomorphisms in degrees
*\leq\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)-1

\pi_{*}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d}))_{(\ell)}\cong\pi_{*+p}(\operatorname{hofib}(G(d-p)/O(d-p)\to G/O))_{(\ell)}\oplus\bigoplus_{m\geq 2}\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)},

and if
\phi=\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)
, there is also an exact sequence of abelian groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bigoplus_{m\geq 2}\pi_{\phi}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\phi}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d}))_{(\ell)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\phi+p}(\operatorname{hofib}(G(d-p)/O(d-p)\to G/O))_{(\ell)},}

where
A_{(\ell)}
denotes
A\otimes{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}_{(\ell)}
, for
A
an abelian group. It remains to understand the groups
\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}
, which are easier to study when
\ell
is coprime to
m
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
m\geq 2
and
d-p\geq 3
. For
\ell\nmid 2m
a prime,

•

if
d
is even and
p
is even, then

\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\pi_{*-m(d-p-2)}^{s}\otimes{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}_{(\ell)},&m=3,5,7,\dots\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

•

if
d
is odd and
p
is odd, then

\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\pi_{*-m(d-p-2)}^{s}\otimes{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}_{(\ell)},&m=2,4,6,\dots\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

•

if
d
is even and
p
is odd, then

\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\pi_{*-m(d-p-2)}^{s}\otimes{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}_{(\ell)},&m=5,9,13,\dots\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

•

if
d
is odd and
p
is even, then

\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}\pi_{*-m(d-p-2)}^{s}\otimes{\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{Z}$}}}_{(\ell)},&m=3,7,11,\dots\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Remark 6.6

(Rational homotopy of spaces of long knots)
.

The rational homology and homotopy of
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
for
d-p\geq 3
has been extensively studied in recent years (see e.g.
[
Tur10
,
AT14
,
AT15
]
) through the lens of embedding calculus and its relation to the little disks operads and their formality, finally culminating in the work of Fresse–Turchin–Willwacher
[
FTW17
]
. There they compute the rational homotopy groups of
\overline{\operatorname{Emb}}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d}):=\operatorname{hofib}_{\iota}(\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})\to\mathrm{Imm}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d}))
as the homology of the
hairy graph complex
(shifted appropriately). Observationally, our results correspond to the
0
- and
1
-loop order parts of this graph complex up to degree
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)\geq 2d-p-5
, where higher loop orders are still not seen. More precisely, the
0
-loop part corresponds to the rational homotopy of
G/G(d-p)
, the lowest summand (i.e.
m=1
when
d-p
is even) of the
1
-loop part appears as that of
O/O(d-p)
, and the higher summands of the
1
-loop part come from the rational homotopy of the spectra
\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}}
for
m\geq 2
, which we just computed. It is worth noting that:

•

The first non-trivial rational homotopy group of
\operatorname{Emb}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})
coming from the
2
-loop part of the hairy graph complex lies in degree
2d-p-4
when both
d
and
p
are odd (cf.
[
FTW17
, Eq. 3]
). Therefore, the lower bound
\phi_{C\mathrm{Emb}}(d,p)\geq 2d-p-5
on the concordance stable range of Goodwillie–Krannich–Kupers is very much sharp.

•

The
1
-loop part of the hairy graph complex seems to be completely generated by the spectra
\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}}
for
m\geq 2
in all degrees outside of the concordance embedding stable range. In other words, the computations in
[
FTW17
]
give evidence for the existence of a rational left splitting of the Weiss–Williams map

\Phi^{\operatorname{Emb}}:\operatorname{Emb}^{(\sim)}_{\partial}(D^{p},D^{d})\longrightarrow\prod_{m\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.85}{$\displaystyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\textstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptstyle\geq$}}{\scalebox{0.85}{$\scriptscriptstyle\geq$}}2}\Omega^{\infty}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}}).

To investigate this, one should first understand the attachment of the second orthogonal derivative
\Theta F^{(2)}
of the orthogonal functor
F(U):=\operatorname{Emb}^{b}_{\partial}(D^{p}\times U,D^{d}\times U)
to its Taylor tower (
2.1
). It would also be interesting to understand the integral picture.

Proof of Proposition
6.5
.

When
\ell
is coprime to
2m
, we have that

\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}\cong H_{0}(D_{m};\pi_{*}^{s}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}))_{(\ell)}

by the homotopy fixed point spectral sequence, because the higher group homology of
D_{m}
is
2m
-torsion. Let
t
and
r
denote the generators of
D_{m}
with
t^{m}=e
and
rtr=t^{-1}
such that

\displaystyle\psi_{m}(t):

\displaystyle\ {\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{m}\ni(a_{1},\dots,a_{m})\longmapsto(a_{m},a_{1},\dots,a_{m-1}),

\displaystyle\psi_{m}(r):

\displaystyle\ {\mathchoice{\scalebox{0.97}{$\displaystyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\textstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}{\scalebox{0.97}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbb{R}$}}}^{m}\ni(a_{1},\dots,a_{m})\longmapsto(a_{m},a_{m-1},\dots,a_{1}).

Then
t
and
r
act on the group
\pi^{s}_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{(d+1)(\sigma-1)+\psi_{m}\otimes(d-p-3+\sigma)})
by
(-1)^{\epsilon_{t}}
and
(-1)^{\epsilon_{r}}
, respectively, where

\displaystyle\epsilon_{t}=(m-1)(d-p-2),\qquad\epsilon_{r}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{d+1}_{(1)}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2}m(m-1)(d-p-3)}_{(2)}+\underbrace{m+\frac{1}{2}m(m-1)}_{(3)}

\displaystyle\equiv d+1+m+\frac{1}{2}m(m-1)(d-p-2)\mod 2.

The terms (
1
), (
2
) and (
3
) are the contributions coming, respectively, from the summands
(d+1)(\sigma-1)
,
\psi_{m}\otimes(d-p-3)
and
\psi_{m}\otimes\sigma
of
\rho_{m}
. One then readily verifies that the groups
H_{0}(D_{m};\pi^{s}_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}))
are given by the fomuli in the statement.
∎

A bit more interesting are the homotopy groups
\pi^{s}_{*}(\mathbb{S}^{\rho_{m}}_{hD_{m}})_{(\ell)}
when
\ell
is odd but divides
m
. We treat the case when
\ell=m=3
, which hopefully serves as a sample computation for other cases.

of the spectrum
